Frayed Demazure weaves for Poisson-compatible cluster structures on Bott–Samelson charts
Jon Cheah

Abstract
. Demazure weaves are combinatorial representations of maps between Bott–Samelson cells and have been used to construct cluster structures on braid varieties. We show the compatibility of these maps and the resulting cluster structures with the standard Poisson structure on the Bott–Samelson variety. Adding frayed strands to Demazure weaves, we further construct Poisson compatible cluster structures on other affine charts of the Bott–Samelson variety in a manner that transition functions across charts become rational quasi-cluster. The mutation sequences we construct for these quasi-cluster morphisms are closely related to those of Ménard for open Richardson seeds.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Scientific context

Cluster algebras were introduced by Fomin and Zelevinsky
[
15
]
as a means to study canonical bases in quantum groups and total positivity in algebraic varieties. It is a natural question whether varieties arising from Lie theory possess cluster structures compatible with total positivity and Poisson geometry. Open Richardson varieties were introduced by Kazhdan–Lusztig, and are useful in studying both the total positivity
[
29
]
and Poisson geometry
[
26
]
of the flag variety.

For a simply-laced algebraic group
G
, Leclerc
[
27
]
used modules of the preprojective algebra to construct a cluster subalgebra in the coordinate ring of open Richardson varieties in the flag variety. It was conjectured that this subalgebra is equal to the whole coordinate ring and proved in some special cases. The conjecture was refined by Ménard
[
30
]
who provided a candidate initial seed, and it was shown in
[
4
]
that this seed defines an upper cluster algebra structure the coordinate ring.

In the finite-type case, open Richardson varieties are special cases of braid varieties. Two separate groups independently resolved Leclerc’s conjecture by constructing cluster algebra structures on braid varieties. Casals–Gorsky–Gorsky–Le–Shen–Simental
[
6
]
used the combinatorics of
Demazure weaves
and Galashin–Lam–Sherman-Bennett–Speyer
[
17
,
18
]
used 3D plabic graphs and Deodhar geometry. These two cluster structures coincide. A comparison of their tori, cluster variables, and Weil–Petersson forms is presented in
[
5
]
.

Demazure weave combinatorics were a main inspiration for this paper. Weaves were introduced in
[
9
]
to study Lagrangian filings of Legendrian links. They returned in
[
7
]
and were used to construct many decompositions of braid varieties. A subclass of weaves, called
Demazure weaves
, correspond to those whose unique strata of maximal dimension are isomorphic to an algebraic torus, and these form the initial cluster tori in
[
6
]
.

Recently, Bao and Ye
[
1
]
demonstrated that Kac–Moody open Richardson varieties admit an upper cluster algebra structure with an initial seed obtained by a generalised version of Ménard’s algorithm. Whether this coincides with the cluster algebra in this generalised setting is still unknown.

1.2.
Poisson compatibility

A cluster structure and a Poisson structure on an algebra are
compatible

[
21
,
22
]
if each every cluster is comprised of mutually of log-canonical elements. In
[
22
]
, it was shown that the cluster algebra structure in
[
2
]
constructed on double Bruhat cells in semisimple algebraic groups is compatible with (the restriction of) the
standard Poisson structure
.

The previous works
[
34
]
and
[
6
]
also construct cluster Poisson structures on Bott–Samelson cells and braid varieties respectively. However, the Poisson structure arising from this need not be the standard Poisson structure from the algebraic group
G
, see
[
34
, Remark 1.11]
.

1.3.
Bott–Samelson charts

Braid varieties and open Richardson varieties are locally-closed subvarieties of an iterated
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration called a Bott–Samelson variety.
An
\ell
-dimensional Bott–Samelson variety
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}
has
2^{\ell}
natural affine coordinate charts which are indexed by subexpressions of the word
\underline{w}
.
In
[
10
]
, Elek and Lu explicitly computed the standard Poisson structure on
Z_{\underline{w}}
in the natural
Bott–Samelson coordinates
of these charts. It was shown that these coordinates form an iterated
T
-Poisson Ore extension, and that for some particular subexpressions, they are in fact symmetric Poisson CGL extensions. A result of Goodearl and Yakimov
[
25
]
says that when this occurs, there exist cluster structures compatible with the Poisson structure.

1.4.
Structure of the paper

Sections
2
, and
3
set up the background on cluster algebra theory and the algebriac varieties that we study.
In Section
4
, we introduce Demazure weaves from
[
6
]
and their associated toric charts. We show that the component maps associated to weave vertices are Poisson maps, and prove in Theorem
4.13
that the corresponding weave tori on braid varieties (and Bott–Samelson cells) are log-canonical in the standard Poisson structure. By considering the initial torus inside the opposite Bott–Samelson chart,
we show the compatibility of the Poisson coefficient matrix and the exchange matrix in Corollary
4.20
.
In Section
5
, we modify weave combinatorics for the other Bott–Samelson charts. New weave vertices correspond to natural Poisson maps between Bott–Samelson charts. These Poisson maps allow us to pull back a seed from the supported subword and give a Poisson compatible cluster structure on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
in Theorem
5.11
.
In Section
6
, we show that the constructed cluster algebra structure on the chart
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
is compatible with the existing one on
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
in the sense that the transition functions become rational quasi-cluster. We show that the initial seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma)
is quasi-equivalent to a freezing of a seed mutation equivalent to the initial seed
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
of
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
.
We define a natural mutation sequence
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}
and describe
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
as an iterated pullback of seeds. We show that this sequence is closely related to Ménard’s mutation sequence
[
30
,
1
]
, and that the seeds for the chart
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
and the open Richardson
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}^{v}
complementary in
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
.
Running examples are provided throughout the work.

In the Appendices
A
and
B
, we broaden the definition of a frayed weave including all possible vertices on a rank
2
simply-laced root subsystem. We list the corresponding braid matrix identities and check the Poisson compatibility of the maps. Some frayed weave equivalences are given based on weaves giving the same moduli of flags, and we explore other embeddings of flag moduli arising from isotopies of fraying vertices.

1.5.
Acknowledgements

We thank Jiang-Hua Lu for helpful discussions on the standard Poisson structure and York Ye for explanations of Ménard’s mutation sequence.
The author is supported by the HKU Presidential PhD Scholar Programme (HKU-PS).

2.
Background on cluster algebras

Throughout, let
J
be a finite index set.

Definition 2.1
.

A
seed
is a quadruple
\mathbf{s}=\bigl((A_{j})_{j\in J},J_{\mathrm{fr}},E,D\bigr)
consisting of:

•

A transcendental basis
(A_{j})_{j\in J}
of a field
K=\mathbb{C}(x_{j})_{j\in J}
, called an
extended cluster
.

•

A subset
J_{\mathrm{fr}}\subset J
. The indices
j\in J_{\mathrm{fr}}
and variables
(A_{j})_{j\in J_{\mathrm{fr}}}
are called
frozen
. Indices and variables in the complement
J_{\mathrm{uf}}=J\setminus J_{\mathrm{fr}}
are called
mutable
or
unfrozen
.

•

An
exchange matrix

E=(\varepsilon_{ij})_{i,j\in J}
, which is a skew-symmetrisable matrix with
\varepsilon_{ij}\in\mathbb{Q}
if both
i,j
are frozen, and
\varepsilon_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}
otherwise.

•

A symmetriser for
D=\mathrm{diag}(d_{1},\dots,d_{\ell})
such that
DE
is skew-symmetric. We may assume that
D
has coprime integer entries.

Given a seed
\mathbf{s}_{0}
and mutable index
k
, its
mutation
in direction
k
gives a seed
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
with the same symmetriser and frozen subset, but with exchange matrix
E^{\prime}=(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{ij})_{i,j\in J}
where

\varepsilon^{\prime}_{ij}=\begin{cases}-\varepsilon_{ij}\quad&\text{ if }k\in\{i,j\},\\
\varepsilon_{ij}+\varepsilon_{ik}\varepsilon_{kj}\quad&\text{ if }k\notin\{i,j\},\text{ and }\varepsilon_{ik},\varepsilon_{kj}>0,\\
\varepsilon_{ij}-\varepsilon_{ik}\varepsilon_{kj}\quad&\text{ if }k\notin\{i,j\},\text{ and }\varepsilon_{ik},\varepsilon_{kj}<0,\\
\varepsilon_{ij},&\text{ else}.\end{cases}

and whose variables are determined by
A_{i}^{\prime}=A_{i}
for each
i\neq k
, and

(1)

A_{k}A_{k}^{\prime}={\prod_{\varepsilon_{ik}<0}A_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{ik}}+\prod_{\varepsilon_{jk}>0}A_{j}^{\varepsilon_{jk}}}.

Remark 2.2
.

Some works including
[
34
,
6
,
1
]
take a different convention for the mutation formula (
1
) where the exponents instead read from the rows of the exchange matrix. The column convention used here aligns with
[
15
,
3
,
12
,
20
]
.

We write
\mathbf{s}\sim\mathbf{s}_{0}
when
\mathbf{s}
can be obtained from
\mathbf{s}_{0}
by a finite sequence of mutations. Given a seed
\mathbf{s}_{0}
, and a subset
\mathrm{inv}\subset J_{\mathrm{fr}}
of frozen indices, we define the
cluster algebra

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathrm{inv})=\mathbb{C}[A_{j}]_{j\in J,\mathbf{s}\sim\mathbf{s}_{0}}[A_{j}^{-1}]_{j\in\mathrm{inv}}
to be the the
\mathbb{C}
-subalgebra of
K
generated by the (cluster and frozen) variables over all mutation-reachable seeds
\mathbf{s}\sim\mathbf{s}_{0}
, as well as the inverses of the variables indexed by
j\in\mathrm{inv}
.
When
\mathrm{inv}=J_{\mathrm{fr}}
, we write

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}_{0}):=\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}_{0},J_{\mathrm{fr}})=\bigl(\mathbb{C}[A_{j}^{\pm 1}]_{j\in J_{\mathrm{fr}}}\bigr)[A_{j,\mathbf{s}}]_{i\in J_{\mathrm{uf}},\mathbf{s}\sim\mathbf{s}_{0}},

and we have
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\emptyset)\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathrm{inv})\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}_{0})
.
By construction, mutation equivalent seeds yield equal cluster algebras. The
upper cluster algebra

\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{s}_{0})=\bigcap_{\mathbf{s}\sim\mathbf{s}_{0}}\mathbb{C}[A_{j,\mathbf{s}}^{\pm 1}]_{j\in J}

is the intersection of the Laurent polynomial rings over each cluster. Mutation equivalent seeds yield the same upper cluster algebra. If we further define

\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathrm{inv})=\bigcap_{\mathbf{s}\sim\mathbf{s}_{0}}\mathbb{C}[A_{j,\mathbf{s}}^{\pm 1}]_{j\in J_{\mathrm{uf}}}[A_{j}]_{j\in J_{\mathrm{fr}}}[A_{j}^{-1}]_{j\in\mathrm{inv}},

then for any
\mathrm{inv}\subset J_{\mathrm{fr}}
, we have

\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\emptyset)\subseteq\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathrm{inv})\subseteq\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{s}_{0})
.
The celebrated
Laurent phenomenon
from
[
15
]
says that
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\emptyset)\subseteq\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\emptyset)
, and therefore
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathrm{inv})\subseteq\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathrm{inv})
for any
\mathrm{inv}\subseteq J_{\mathrm{fr}}
. It is of general interest in which situations equality is achieved.

We will also make use of the notion of
quasi-equivalence
of seeds, first introduced in
[
16
]
, which give a more flexible criterion for when two seeds give the same cluster algebra.

Definition 2.3
.

Two seeds
\mathbf{s}=\bigl((A_{j})_{j\in J},J_{\mathrm{fr}},B,D\bigr)
and
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}=\bigl((A^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in J^{\prime}},J^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fr}},B^{\prime},D^{\prime}\bigr)
with cluster variables in the same field
K
are
quasi-equivalent
if we have:

(1)

A bijection
\phi:J\to J^{\prime}
such that
\phi(J_{\mathrm{fr}})=J^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fr}}
, and
d_{\phi(j)}=d^{\prime}_{j}
.

(2)

The frozen variables of
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
generate the same multiplicative subgroup of
K
.

(3)

For each
j\in J_{\mathrm{uf}}
, the cluster variables
A
and
A_{\phi(j)}^{\prime}
differ by a Laurent monomial of frozen variables.

(4)

For each
j\in J_{\mathrm{uf}}
, the
exchange ratios

y_{j}=\frac{\prod_{\varepsilon_{ij}>0}A_{i}^{\varepsilon_{ij}}}{\prod_{\varepsilon_{ij}<0}A_{i}^{-\varepsilon_{ij}}}=\prod_{i}A_{i}^{\varepsilon_{ij}},

are preserved. That is,
y_{i}=y_{\phi(i)}^{\prime}
for each
i
.

Remark 2.4
.

Preservation of the exchange ratios is the necessary condition for quasi-equivalences to commute with mutation. This can be equivalently thought of as the
2
-forms agreeing on the respective tori, see
[
18
, Definition 3.8]
.

Lemma 2.5
.

If seeds
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
are quasi-equivalent, then for any mutable index
j
,
\mu_{j}(s)
and
\mu_{\phi(j)}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})
are quasi equivalent.

Corollary 2.6
.

Quasi-equivalent seeds yield the same cluster algebra.

2.1.
Operations on seeds

Given a seed
\mathbf{s}
, its
freezing
at
i\in J_{\mathrm{uf}}
, is the seed
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}=\bigl((A_{j})_{j\in J},J_{\mathrm{fr}}\cup\{i\},B,D\bigr)
.

Lemma 2.7

(
[
31
, Proposition 3.1]
)
.

If
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathbf{s}
by freezing at
i\in J
, we have

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s})[A_{i,\mathbf{s}}^{-1}]\subseteq\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{s})[A_{i,\mathbf{s}}^{-1}]\subseteq\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime}),

as subalgebras of the ambient field
K
.

Given a seed
\mathbf{s}
, we can
delete
an index
i\in J
by considering the same seed supported on the index set
J\setminus\{i\}
.
That is, we consider the seed

\mathbf{s}^{\dagger}=\bigl((A_{j})_{j\in J\setminus\{i\}},J_{\mathrm{fr}}\setminus\{i\},(\varepsilon_{jk})_{j,k\neq i},(d_{j})_{j\neq i}\bigr).

Lemma 2.8

(
[
31
, Proposition 3.7]
)
.

If
\mathbf{s}^{\dagger}
is obtained from
\mathbf{s}
by deleting a frozen vertex
i\in J_{\mathrm{fr}}
, then we have natural isomorphisms

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s})/(A_{i}-1)\cong\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}^{\dagger})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{s})/(A_{i}-1)\cong\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{s}^{\dagger}).

A variety
X
over
\mathbb{C}
has
a cluster structure
if its coordinate ring
\mathbb{C}[X]
is the cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s},\mathrm{inv})
for some initial seed
\mathbf{s}
and inversion set
\mathrm{inv}\subseteq J_{\mathrm{fr}}
. It has an
upper cluster structure
if
\mathbb{C}[X]\cong\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{s},\mathrm{inv})
.

2.2.
Compatible Poisson brackets

Definition 2.9
.

A
Poisson bracket

\{\cdot,\cdot\}:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
on a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
is a Lie bracket which is a
\mathbb{C}
-derivation in both entries.

Poisson brackets readily extend to the field of fractions
\mathrm{Frac}(\mathcal{A})
.
A
Poisson structure
on an algebraic variety
X
will be a Poisson bracket on its ring of regular functions or equivalently its field of rational functions. This is equivalent to the data of a
Poisson bivector field

\pi\in\Gamma(X,\mbox{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}TX)
satisfying the Schouten bracket condition
[\pi,\pi]=0
.
Elements
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
are
log-canonical
with respect to the bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
if
\{f_{i},f_{j}\}=\omega_{ij}f_{i}f_{j}
, for some structure constants
\omega_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition 2.10

(
[
21
]
)
.

Given a cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}_{0})
, a Poisson structure on the ambient field
\mathcal{F}
is
compatible
if and only if all extended clusters are log-canonical.

Lemma 2.11

(
[
21
, Theorem 1.4, Equation (1.5)]
, see also
[
3
, Definition 3.1]
)
.

Let
\Omega=(\omega_{ij})
be the Poisson coefficient matrix of a log-canonical initial cluster with a (tall) extended exchange matrix
\widetilde{E}=E[I_{{\mathrm{uf}}}\;0]^{T}
given by the mutable columns of
E
. Then the Poisson structure is compatible if and only if
\widetilde{E}^{T}\Omega=[\Delta\;\;0]
, for
\Delta
a diagonal matrix.

While a cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variety possesses a natural cluster–Poisson structure, this might in principle be a different Poisson structure to other known Poisson structures. See Remark 1.11 in
[
34
]
. The vector space of Poisson structures compatible with a given cluster structure is described in
[
21
, Theorem 1.4]
.

2.3.
Morphisms between cluster algebras

Definition 2.12

(
[
16
]
)
.

A
quasi-cluster homomorphism
is an algebra homomorphism
\psi:\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s})\to\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})
between cluster algebras of the same rank (i.e.
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
have same number of mutable variables) such that for every
j\in J_{\mathrm{uf}}
,
\psi(A_{j})/A^{\prime}_{j}
is a monomial in frozens variables, the image of a frozen variable is a monomial of frozen variables and exchange ratios are preserved.

In other words, quasi-equivalent seeds arise from quasi-cluster homomorphisms between cluster algebras with a quasi-cluster inverse.

Definition 2.13

(
[
1
, Definition 4.1]
)
.

Suppose we have an embedding
\sigma:K\to K^{\prime}
, cluster algebras
\mathcal{A}\subset K
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subset K^{\prime}
with initial seeds
\mathbf{s}
,
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
respectively.
If we have a subset
J_{\mathrm{sup}}\subseteq J
containing the mutable indices,
J_{\mathrm{uf}}\subseteq J_{\mathrm{sup}}
, an injection
\iota:J_{\mathrm{sup}}\hookrightarrow J^{\prime}
such that
\iota(J_{\mathrm{uf}})\subseteq J_{\mathrm{uf}}^{\prime}
, and a subset
S^{\prime}\subseteq J^{\prime}\setminus\iota(J_{\mathrm{sup}})
, which satisfy the following three properties:

(1)

for any
i\in J_{\mathrm{sup}}
, we have
\sigma(A_{i})=A^{\prime}_{\iota(i)}M_{i}
, where
M_{i}
is a Laurent monomial in the
A^{\prime}_{j}
, for
j\in S^{\prime}
;

(2)

for any
i\in J_{\mathrm{uf}}
, the exchange ratios satisfy
\sigma(X_{i})=X^{\prime}_{\iota(i)}
, where

X_{i}=\prod_{j\in J}A_{j}^{\varepsilon_{ij}}\quad\text{and}\quad X^{\prime}_{\iota(i)}=\prod_{j\in J^{\prime}}(A^{\prime}_{j})^{\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\iota(i)j}};

(3)

for any
i\in J\setminus J_{\mathrm{sup}}
,
\sigma(A_{i})
is a Laurent monomial in the
A^{\prime}_{j}
, for
j\in S^{\prime}
;

Then
\sigma:K\hookrightarrow K^{\prime}
is called
rational quasi-cluster
, and is denoted by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma:(\mathcal{A},\mathbf{s})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{\mathrm{sup}}{,}S^{\prime})}

In cases when
J_{\mathrm{sup}}=J_{\mathrm{uf}}
and
S^{\prime}=J^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fr}}
, and
|J_{\mathrm{uf}}|=|J_{\mathrm{uf}}^{\prime}|
, this notion coincides with quasi-cluster morphisms from
[
16
]
.
The advantage of rational quasi-cluster maps is that neither ranks (number of mutable variables), nor dimensions are necessarily preserved.

3.
Bott–Samelson (sub-)varieties and charts

3.1.
Notation and conventions

Let
G
be a simple algebraic group. We fix a
pinning
. Fix a pair of opposite Borel subgroups
B
and
B^{-}
, their unipotent radicals
U
and
U^{-}
radicals, and the maximal torus
T=B\cap B^{-}
. The simple roots
\alpha_{i}
are indexed by a set
I
, and the Weyl group
W=N_{G}(T)/T
is generated by simple reflections
s_{i}
for
i\in I
. For each
i\in I
, we fix isomorphisms
u_{i}:\mathbb{A}^{1}\to U_{\alpha_{i}}
and
u_{-i}:\mathbb{A}^{1}\to U_{-\alpha_{i}}
so that

\begin{pmatrix}1&z\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\mapsto u_{i}(z),\qquad\begin{pmatrix}x&0\\
0&x^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x),\qquad\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
z&1\end{pmatrix}\mapsto u_{-i}(z),

defines a homomorphism
\varphi_{i}:SL_{2}\to G
. Our Cartan matrix will be denoted
A=(a_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
such that
\alpha_{i}(\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x))=x^{a_{ij}}
for any
x\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

We will frequently deal with
subexpressions
of a word, which amount to choosing some positions of the word whose letters we omit.
In
[
10
]
, omitted letters in a word of simple reflections were replaced with instances of the identity, so
(s_{1},e,s_{1})
was a subexpression of
(s_{1},s_{2},s_{1})
. In
[
11
]
, these positions were marked with blanks, and this example would be denoted
(s_{1},{-},s_{1})
.

For what follows, it will be necessary to keep track of not just the letters included in the subexpression, but the letters which were removed. We will denote a subexpression
\gamma
of
\underline{w}=(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell})\in I^{\ell}
by an element of the set

\Upsilon_{\underline{w}}=\{i_{1},-i_{1}\}\times\{i_{2},-i_{2}\}\times\cdots\times\{i_{\ell},-i_{\ell}\}.

Positive letters in a subexpression will be called
supported
, and negative letters will be
unsupported
.
For
i\in\pm I
, we define

s_{i}^{+}=\begin{cases}s_{i},\text{ if }i>0,\\
e,\text{ if }i<0.\end{cases}

Now, for a subexpression
\gamma
as a sequence of signed letters, we can obtain the sequence
s_{\gamma}=(s_{i_{1}}^{+},s_{i_{2}}^{+},\dots,s_{i_{\ell}}^{+})
which corresponds to the notation used in
[
10
]
.
For
1\leq k\leq\ell
, we use
\gamma^{k}
to denote the product
s^{+}_{\gamma_{1}}s^{+}_{\gamma_{2}}\cdots s^{+}_{\gamma_{k}}
, i.e. the element in
W
given by the
k
-th partial product of the subexpression.

These signed expressions should not be confused with the various other instances of words in two copies of the same alphabet. In
[
34
]
, negative letters are used to denote opposite flags in an opposite flag variety and their relative positions. In
[
18
]
, negative letters are used to denote relative positions in the same flag variety, but with a reversed direction. Our use of negative letters will be to denote different charts in the same variety, as well as one-parameter subgroups given by the negative roots.

We have the standard lift of the simple reflection

\dot{s}_{i}=u_{i}(-1)u_{-i}(1)u_{i}(-1)=\varphi_{i}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in N_{G}(T).

For an element
w\in W
of the Weyl group with reduced expression
w=s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell}}
, its standard lift
\dot{w}=\dot{s}_{i_{1}}\dot{s}_{i_{2}}\cdots\dot{s}_{i_{\ell}}
is known to be independent of choice of reduced expression.

If
\underline{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell})
is an
\ell
-tuple of invertible elements in a ring and
c=(c_{1},\dots,c_{\ell})^{T}
is a column vector of integers, we write
\underline{x}^{c}=x_{1}^{c_{1}}\cdots x_{\ell}^{c_{\ell}}
. If
C=(c_{ij})
is an
\ell\times m
integer matrix with columns
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}
, we write
\underline{x}^{C}=(\underline{x}^{C_{1}},\dots\underline{x}^{C_{m}})
.

3.2.
Bott–Samelsons, bricks, cells, and braids

We now introduce various subvarieties of the twisted product of flags

G\times^{B}G\times^{B}\cdots\times^{B}G/B,

including Bott–Samelson varieties, brick manifolds, braid varieties, and Bott–Samelson charts.
Flags in
G/B
will be denoted by calligraphic letters
\mathcal{B}\in G/B
, or by coset representatives
gB\in G/B
. Two flags
xB,yB
are in relative position
w\in W
if and only if
x^{-1}yB\in BwB
, and this will be denoted by
xB\xrightarrow{w}yB
.

For a
i\in I
, let
P_{i}
denote the parabolic subgroup
P_{i}=B\sqcup Bs_{i}B\subset G.
For a word
\underline{w}=({i_{1}},{i_{2}},\dots,{i_{\ell}})\in I^{\ell}
, consider the product variety
P_{\underline{w}}:=P_{i_{1}}\times P_{i_{2}}\times\cdots\times P_{i_{\ell}}\subset G^{\ell}
and endow it with a (right)
B^{\ell}
-action given by

(2)

(g_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{\ell})*(b_{1},\dots b_{\ell})=(g_{1}b_{1},b_{1}^{-1}g_{2}b_{2},\dots,b_{l-1}^{-1}g_{\ell}b_{\ell}).

The
Bott–Samelson variety
associated to
\underline{w}
is the quotient space

Z_{\underline{w}}:=P_{\underline{w}}/B^{\ell}
.
It is endowed with the structure of a projective variety via the
B^{\ell}
-equivariant embedding

Z_{\underline{w}}\hookrightarrow(G/B)^{\ell},\quad[g_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{\ell}]\mapsto\bigl(g_{1}B,g_{1}g_{2}B,\dots,g_{1}{\cdots}\,g_{\ell}B\bigr),

so we can equivalently view the Bott–Samelson variety as comprised of sequences of flags,

Z_{\underline{w}}=\left\{(\mathcal{B}_{0},\mathcal{B}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{B}_{\ell})\in(G/B)^{\ell+1}\;\middle|\;\mathcal{B}_{0}=B\text{, and }\mathcal{B}_{j-1}=\mathcal{B}_{j}\text{ or }\mathcal{B}_{j-1}\xrightarrow{s_{i_{j}}}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right\}.

Note that each flag
\mathcal{B}_{j}
is chosen out of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of possible flags which differ from
\mathcal{B}_{j}
in the
i_{j}
component. This gives the Bott–Samelson variety the structure of an iterated
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration.
The multiplication map
m:Z_{\underline{w}}\to G/B
is given by
[g_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{\ell}]\mapsto g_{1}g_{2}\cdots g_{\ell}B
. For sequences of flags this is projection onto the last entry. T

The
brick manifold

[
11
]
is the closed subvariety
m^{-1}(\delta(\underline{w})B)
, consisting of those sequences whose final flag differs from the initial flag by the Demazure product
\delta(\underline{w})
. When
\delta(\underline{w})=w_{0}
is the longest element, the brick manifold is equal to
m^{-1}(B^{-}w_{0}B/B)\subset Z_{\underline{w}}
.

The
Bott–Samelson cell
is the open subvariety of
{Z_{\underline{w}}}
of distinct consecutive flags:

\displaystyle\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}

\displaystyle=(Bs_{i_{1}}B)\times^{B}\cdots\times^{B}(Bs_{i_{\ell}}B)/B,

\displaystyle=\left\{(\mathcal{B}_{0},\mathcal{B}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{B}_{\ell})\in(G/B)^{\ell+1}\;\middle|\;\mathcal{B}_{0}=B,\mathcal{B}_{j-1}\xrightarrow{s_{i_{j}}}\mathcal{B}_{j}\right\}.

The unique factorisation
Bs_{i}B=U_{\alpha_{i}}\dot{s}_{i}B
, where
U_{\alpha_{i}}
is the
1
-parameter subgroup associated to the simple root
\alpha
, can be used to iteratively trivialise the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration from the left to right.
This gives an isomorphism to an affine space of dimension
\ell
. When
\underline{w}
is a reduced word, the
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}
is isomorphic to the Schubert cell
\mathring{X_{w}}
where
w=\delta(\underline{w})
.

The intersection of the brick manifold with the Bott–Samelson cell
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}\cap m^{-1}(\delta(\underline{w})B)
is called the
braid variety
. In
[
7
,
6
]
, these are denoted by
X(\beta)
where
\beta
is the class positive braid words for
\underline{w}
. This is because braid moves on words give natural isomorphisms between the Bott–Samelson cells which respect the multiplication map, and so words related by braid moves give isomorphic varieties.
It is shown in
[
6
]
that letters which strictly increase the Demazure product can be added to the word without changing the isomorphism class of the variety. When working in finite type, there are (usually) no issues assuming that
\delta(\underline{w})=w_{0}
.

For
v\in W
such that
v\leq\delta(\underline{w})
, we define the
open Richardson variety

\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}^{v}=\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}\cap m^{-1}(B^{-}vB/B)
inside the twisted product of flags. When
\underline{w}
is a reduced word, the isomorphism of the Bott–Samelson cell to the Schubert cell
B_{w}B/B
gives an isomorphism of the
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}^{v}
with the usual open Richardson varieties
\mathring{R}_{w}^{v}\subseteq G/B
in the flag variety.

When
\delta(\underline{w})=w_{0}
, we have braid variety as an open Richardson variety

X(\underline{w})=\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}^{w_{0}}=\left\{(\mathcal{B}_{0},\mathcal{B}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{B}_{\ell})\in(G/B)^{\ell+1}\;\middle|\;\mathcal{B}_{0}=B,\mathcal{B}_{j-1}\xrightarrow{s_{i_{j}}}\mathcal{B}_{j},\mathcal{B}_{\ell}=w_{0}B\right\}.

Remark 3.1
.

The above describes the braid variety as closed variety inside the Bott–Samelson cell corresponding to the same word. There is also an isomorphism (see
[
6
, Lemma 3.16]
) between an open subset of a Bott–Samelson cell and a braid variety of a longer word. Specifically,
X((\underline{w_{0}},\underline{w}))
is isomorphic to
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}\cap m^{-1}({B^{-}B/B})=\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}^{e}.

The complement of this open set is the union of
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}^{s_{i}}=\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}\cap m^{-1}({B^{-}s_{i}B/B})
where the
s_{i}
range over all simple reflections. Equivalently, the open set is the non-vanishing of the generalised minors
[
14
, §1.4]

\Delta_{\omega_{i},\omega_{i}}
evaluated on the the final flag
\mathcal{B}_{\ell}
.

This isomorphism is used in
[
6
]
to show that their cluster structure on braid varieties agrees with that on the Bott–Samelson cell constructed in
[
34
]
.

3.3.
The standard seed for the Bott–Samelson cell

A particular initial seed for the Bott–Samelson cell
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}=\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
is ubiquitous in the literature. The quivers appear in
[
20
]
as endomorphism algebras of preprojective algebra modules in their additive categorification of Schubert cells. It comes from the
right inductive weave

[
6
, Definition 4.5]
from for the braid variety
X(\underline{w_{0}},\underline{w})
, which agrees by
[
6
, §4.8]
with the initial seed from
[
34
]
constructed by amalgamation. The cluster torus is the classical Deodhar torus from the Deodhar decomposition of the flag variety, which gives the first seed in
[
18
]
.
In
[
28
]
, it is shown that this is also the standard initial seed arising from the procedure in
[
25
]
for the symmetric Poisson CGL presentation on
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
.
In the case that
\underline{w}
is a reduced word of the longest Grassmannian element in type
A_{n}
, this is the
rectangles seed
considered in
[
32
,
33
,
13
]
comprised of the Plücker coordinates whose young tableaux presentations are given by rectangles. This last example is particularly interesting since Grassmannian elements are
fully commutative

[
35
]
, so all choices of reduced words yield this particular cluster.

Here, we describe the construction of the quiver for the seed
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
on
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}
by the amalgamation procedure used in
[
34
]
and
[
1
]
.
Given two exchange matrices and a bijection between subsets of their respective frozen vertices, their
amalgamation
is obtained by identifying this subset of frozens.

More precisely, suppose we have exchange matrices
E
and
E^{\prime}
whose vertex sets are
J
and
J^{\prime}
respectively, subsets of frozen vertices
J_{1}\subseteq J\setminus J_{uf}
and
J_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq J^{\prime}\setminus J_{uf}^{\prime}
, and a bijection
\phi:J_{1}\to J_{1}^{\prime}
such that
d_{i}=d_{\phi(i)}
for every
i\in J_{1}
. Then the amalgamation has vertex set
J^{\prime\prime}:=J\sqcup J^{\prime}/\sim_{\phi}
, unfrozen vertices
J_{uf}^{\prime\prime}=J_{uf}\sqcup J_{uf}^{\prime}
, and is such that
E^{\prime\prime}=E+E^{\prime}
, where the matrices have been first extended by zeroes. The condition that the symmetrisers agree at the vertices identified by
\phi
ensures the new matrix is skew–symmetrisable with the combined symmetriser.

We now explicitly construct the seed
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
for a word
\underline{w}
in the roots of a root system with Cartan matrix
A=(a_{ij})
and symmetriser
D=\mathrm{diag}(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
such that
AD
is symmetric.

For a simple reflection
i\in I
, define the exchange matrix
E(i)
as follows:
Its vertex set is
J(i)=\{j_{0}\mid j\in I\setminus\{i\}\}\sqcup\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
and all vertices are frozen. The exchange matrix is given by
\varepsilon_{i_{0},i_{1}}=-1
and
\varepsilon_{i_{0},j_{0}}=-\frac{a_{ji}}{2}
and
\varepsilon_{j_{0},i_{1}}=\frac{a_{ji}}{2}
for
j\neq i
, and
\varepsilon_{j_{0}k_{0}}=0
for
j,k\neq i
.

Remark 3.2
.

Our convention for the seed here differs from
[
34
,
6
,
1
]
by a transpose of the Cartan matrix. This is to align with our mutation formula in (
1
), see also Remark
2.2
.

The symmetrisers are the
d_{j}
’s coming from the symmetriser of the Cartan matrix where both
i_{l},i_{r}\in J(i)
are treated as
i
.
The exchange matrix
E(\underline{w})
is then constructed inductively amalgamating
E(i)
for the letters making up
\underline{w}
, and defrosting one vertex each time. To be precise, let
n_{i}
be the number of times
i
appears in
\underline{w}
, and say
E(\underline{w})
has vertex set
J(\underline{w})=\{(i,l)\mid i\in I,0\leq l\leq n_{i}\}
and unfrozen vertices
J(\underline{w})_{uf}=\{(i,l)\mid i\in I,0<l<n_{i}\}
. Note
E(i)
is of this form. Then to form
E(\underline{w},i)
we identify the frozen vertices
(j,n_{j})\in J(\underline{w})
with
j_{0}\in E(i)
, rename the vertex
i_{1}
as
(i,n_{i}+1)
, and defrost the vertex
(i,n_{i})
.
For seeds on the Bott–Samelson cell, we will delete the frozen vertices of indexed by
(i,0)
.

The effect of this is that we have
n_{i}
vertices of the form
(i,l)
which corresponds the
l
-th instance of
i
in
\underline{w}
. We therefore have two ways of indexing the vertices of
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
, and will alternate between them depending on context. Explicitly, if
i=i_{j}
is the
j
-th position of
\underline{w}
and is the
m
-th appearance of
i
, then we refer to that vertex both as
j
and as
(i,m)
. This amalgamation procedure results in a constructive/destructive interference phenomenon for the half-weighted indices between two frozen vertices, which results in integer entries after each defrosted stage.
One useful property of these exchange matrices is that each frozen vertex is adjacent to exactly one mutable vertex.

The amalgamated exchange matrix can be described by an interlacing property (see also
[
2
,
20
]
). For a word
\underline{w}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell})
, we define the
predecessor
and
successor
functions

(3)

p(j)=\begin{cases}\max\{k<j\mid i_{k}=i_{j}\},&\text{if }\exists k<j\text{ with }i_{k}=i_{j},\\
-\infty,&\text{else},\end{cases}

and

(4)

s(j)=\begin{cases}\min\{k>j\mid i_{k}=i_{j}\},&\text{if }\exists k>j\text{ with }i_{k}=i_{j},\\
\infty,&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Then the entries of the exchange matrix
E(\underline{w})
are given by

(5)

\varepsilon_{jk}=\begin{cases}a_{i_{k},i_{j}}&\text{if }k<j<s(k)<s(j),\\
-a_{i_{k},i_{j}}&\text{if }j<k<s(j)<s(k),\\
\frac{a_{i_{k},i_{j}}}{2}&\text{if }k<j<s(k)=s(j)=\infty,\\
-\frac{a_{i_{k},i_{j}}}{2}&\text{if }j<k<s(j)=s(k)=\infty,\\
1&\text{if }j=s(k),\\
-1&\text{if }j=p(k),\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}

The cluster variables of this seed
A_{j}
are given by generalised minors of the partial products
\varphi_{j}=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{j}},\omega_{i_{j}}}\circ m\circ\psi_{\underline{w},j}
.
Specifically, for the
j
-th letter of
\underline{w}
and its vertex
v_{j}=(i_{j},m)
, we have the cluster variable
A_{j}=A_{(i_{j},m)}=\varphi_{j}=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{j}},\omega_{i_{j}}}(B_{\underline{w}[j]}(z_{1},\dots,z_{j}))
for the Bott–Samelson coordinates
z_{i}
on
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
. The frozen variables for the seed are those given by the last instance of each letter in
\underline{w}
and are
\Delta_{\omega_{i},\omega_{i}}(B_{\underline{w}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell}))
for
i\in\mathrm{supp}(\underline{w})
.
Every polynomial generator
z_{j}
is reachable from this seed by a sequence of mutations
[
20
]
(see also
[
34
,
6
,
18
]
) and therefore

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))=\mathbb{C}[\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}\cap m^{-1}(B^{-}B/B)]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}),\emptyset)=\mathbb{C}[\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}].

Remark 3.3
.

Similar exchange matrices are constructed for expressions in two alphabets of simple roots
[
34
]
. Letters
\overline{i}
for
i\in I
are associated to a component matrix
E(\overline{i})
which is the negation of
E(i)
, and these are amalgamated similarly. We reiterate that signed letters in subexpressions
\gamma
have a distinct meaning which we introduce in the next subsection.

3.4.
Bott–Samelson charts

The Bott–Samelson variety is an iterated
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration: at the
j
-th stage we have a new entry in
P_{i_{j}}/B\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Equivalently, under the embedding
Z_{\underline{w}}\hookrightarrow(G/B)^{\ell}
, at each stage, we modify the
i_{j}
-th component of the flag out of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of possible subspaces to get the next flag in the sequence. With this fibration structure, the variety has a number of natural affine charts, of which the Bott–Samelson cell
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}
is one.

For the Bott–Samelson cell, we imposed conditions
g_{j}B\neq B
, that consecutive flags are not equal. To obtain other charts, we instead use the opposite affine chart in
\mathbb{P}^{1}
in certain positions, and ask that
g_{j}B\neq s_{i_{j}}B
. We index these by the subexpressions
\gamma\in\Upsilon_{\underline{w}}
of
\underline{w}
. In terms of relative positions, when
\gamma_{j}=-s_{i_{j}}
, we have that
\mathcal{B}_{j-1}=\mathcal{B}_{j}
or
\mathcal{B}_{j-1}\xrightarrow{s_{i_{j}}}\mathcal{B}_{j}
, but
\mathcal{B}_{j-1}\neq s_{i_{j}}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{j}
. We can uniformly describe these charts as the images of affine spaces under particular embeddings dependent on the subexpression
\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{\ell})\in\Upsilon_{\underline{w}}
. The
Bott–Samelson chart

\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
is the image of the embedding

\Phi_{\gamma}:\mathbb{A}^{\ell}\to Z_{\underline{w}},\quad(z_{1},\dots,z_{l})\mapsto\left[u_{\gamma_{1}}(z_{1})\dot{s}^{+}_{\gamma_{1}},u_{\gamma_{2}}(z_{2})\dot{s}^{+}_{\gamma_{2}},\dots,u_{\gamma_{\ell}}(z_{\ell})\dot{s}^{+}_{\gamma_{\ell}}\right].

Note that the chart for the “full” subexpression
\gamma=\underline{w}
is equal to the Bott–Samelson cell.

We call two charts
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}

adjacent
, if there is an index
k
such that
\gamma_{j}=\gamma_{j}^{\prime}
for all
j\neq k
and
\gamma_{k}=-\gamma_{k}^{\prime}
. One should picture the
1
-skeleton of an
\ell
-dimensional hypercube with vertices labelled by subexpressions and edges connecting adjacent charts.

Remark 3.4
.

The change of coordinates between any two Bott–Samelson charts can be computed as the composition of a sequence of coordinate changes between adjacent charts.

Definition 3.5
.

For a subexpression
\gamma\in\Upsilon_{\underline{w}}
and
\underline{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell})\in\mathbb{A}^{\ell}
, we define the
braid matrix
to be the element of
G
given by the product

B_{\gamma}(\underline{z})=B_{\gamma}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell}):=u_{\gamma_{1}}(z_{1})\dot{s}^{+}_{\gamma_{1}}u_{\gamma_{2}}(z_{2})\dot{s}^{+}_{\gamma_{2}}\cdots u_{\gamma_{\ell}}(z_{\ell})\dot{s}^{+}_{\gamma_{\ell}}.

This construction is such that
m\circ\Phi_{\gamma}(\underline{z})=B_{\gamma}(\underline{z})\cdot B\in G/B
.

Similar to Remark
3.1
, the fibre of the multiplication map over the longest element can be used to study an open subset of
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
. The following lemma is essentially
[
6
, Lemma 3.16]
adapted for an arbitrary Bott–Samelson.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\gamma
be a signed expression, and
\underline{w_{0}}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{r})\in I^{r}
a reduced expression of the longest element with positive letters. There is a natural isomorphism

\mathcal{O}^{(\underline{w_{0}},\gamma)}\cap m^{-1}(w_{0}B/B)\cong\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\cap m^{-1}(B^{-}B/B).

Proof.

Let
\zeta_{1},\dots\zeta_{N},z_{1},\dots z_{\ell}
denote the coordinates on the Bott–Samelson chart
\mathcal{O}^{(\underline{w_{0}},\gamma)}
, where the
\zeta_{i}
’s correspond to the letters in
\underline{w_{0}}
, and
z_{i}
’s those in
\gamma
. By construction,

m\circ\Phi_{(\underline{w_{0}},\gamma)}(\underline{\zeta},\underline{z})=\dot{w_{0}}B\in G/B\;\text{ if and only if }\;\dot{w_{0}}\cdot B_{\underline{w_{0}}}(\underline{\zeta})B_{\gamma}(\underline{z})\in B.

Note that the map
\underline{\zeta}\mapsto w_{0}\cdot B_{\underline{w_{0}}}(\underline{\zeta})
gives an isomorphism
\mathbb{A}^{N}\to w_{0}Uw_{0}=U^{-}
. Hence,
B_{\gamma}(\underline{z})\in B^{-}B
and
m\circ\Phi_{\gamma}(\underline{z})\in B^{-}B/B
. Conversely, for
\Phi_{\gamma}(\underline{z})\in\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\cap m^{-1}(B^{-}B/B)
, the product
B_{\gamma}(\underline{z})\in U^{-}B
factors uniquely as
B_{\gamma}(\underline{z})=x_{-}x_{+}
with
x_{-}\in U^{-},x_{+}\in B
. For the unique
\underline{\zeta}
such that

\dot{w_{0}}\cdot B_{\underline{w_{0}}}(\underline{\zeta})=(x_{-})^{-1}
, we obtain
\dot{w_{0}}\cdot B_{\underline{w_{0}}}(\underline{\zeta})B_{\gamma}(\underline{z})\in B
as required.
∎

3.5.
Morphisms between Bott–Samelson varieties and their charts

For any subset
J=\{j_{1}<\dots<j_{m}\}\subset[\ell]=\left\{1,\dots,\ell\right\}
, consider
\underline{w}[J]=\left(i_{j_{1}},\dots,i_{j_{m}}\right)
.
There is closed embedding
\iota_{w[J]}:Z_{\underline{w}[J]}\to Z_{\underline{w}}
obtained by “inserting identities in the blank spaces”,

\iota:[g_{1},\dots,g_{\ell}]\mapsto[e,\dots,e,g_{1},e,\dots,e,g_{\ell},e,\dots,e],

where for each
1\leq k\leq m
,
g_{i}
is in position
j_{k}
in the output, and we have the identity element
e
is in all positions in
[\ell]\setminus J
.

For any
1\leq m\leq\ell
, we have the truncated word of length
m
given by
\underline{w}[m]=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
. There is a projection morphism
\psi_{\underline{w},m}:Z_{\underline{w}}\to Z_{\underline{w}[m]}
,
[g_{1},\dots,g_{\ell}]\to[g_{1},\dots,g_{m}]
given by removing the last
\ell-m
entries or equivalently by forgetting the last
\ell-m
flags. For
m=\ell-1
, we abbreviate to
\psi_{\underline{w},\ell-1}
to
\psi_{\underline{w}}
so that

\psi_{\underline{w},m}=\psi_{\underline{w}[m+1]}\circ\cdots\circ\psi_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}\circ\psi_{\underline{w}}.

Each truncation
\psi_{\underline{w},m}
is a locally trivial
Z_{[\ell-m+1,\ell]}
-fibration, with a section
\sigma_{\underline{w},m}=\iota_{\underline{w}[m]}
.

These truncation morphisms have natural restrictions to Bott–Samelson charts, but the embeddings
\iota_{\underline{w}[J]}
only restrict to charts if the target chart is unsupported at all positions in
[\ell]\setminus J
.

Lemma 3.7
.

The truncation map
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}\to\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}[m]}
is a rational quasi-cluster homomorphism.

Proof.

This arises from the embedding of seeds
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}[m])\hookrightarrow\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
. The cluster variables are the same generalised minors of the same partial products, and the mutable parts of the quiver for
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}[m])
agrees with its image in
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
.
∎

3.6.
The standard Poisson structure

A
Poisson algebraic group
is a an algebraic group with a Poisson structure
\pi_{G}
such that the group multiplication

m:(G\times G,\pi_{G}\times\pi_{G})\to(G,\pi_{G})

is a Poisson map.
Every connected complex semi-simple algebraic group can be endowed with its
standard Poisson structure

\pi_{\mathrm{st}}
, which is defined using a choice of opposite Borel subgroups. The standard Poisson structure is multiplicative, and is the semi-classical limit of the associated quantum group.

For a fixed symmetric non-degenerate invariant bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
\mathfrak{g}
(and its induced form on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
), the Poisson bivector for the standard Poisson structure is given by

(6)

\pi_{\mathrm{st}}=\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi_{+}}\frac{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}{2}e_{-\alpha}\wedge e_{\alpha}\in\mbox{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}\mathfrak{g}.

Note that this bivector depends on the choice of non-degenerate bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, but not on the choice of pinning. A canonical choice of this form is such that
\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle=2
for the short roots so that
\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle\in\{2,4,6\}
for all simple roots, so
\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle=a_{ij}d_{j}

The standard Poisson structure on the Bott–Samelson variety
[
10
]
is defined by by taking the product Poisson structure
(\pi_{\mathrm{st}})^{\ell}
on product of Poisson algebraic subgroups
P_{\underline{w}}
and pushing it forward onto the quotient space
Z_{\underline{w}}
. Elek and Lu then calculated the Poisson bracket in the coordinates of each Bott–Samelson chart.

Lemma 3.8

(
[
10
, Lemma 3.1, Theorem 4.14]
)
.

In the Bott–Samelson coordinates, the Poisson structure on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\subset Z_{\underline{w}}
is given by

(7)

\{z_{j},z_{k}\}=\begin{cases}\langle\gamma^{j}(\alpha_{j}),\gamma^{k}(\alpha_{k})\rangle z_{j}z_{k}&\text{ if }\gamma_{j}<0;\\
-\langle\gamma^{j}(\alpha_{j}),\gamma^{k}(\alpha_{k})\rangle z_{j}z_{k}-\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle\sigma_{j}(z_{k})&\text{ if }\gamma_{j}>0,\end{cases}

for
j<k
. Here,
\sigma_{j}(z_{k})
denotes the action of the vector field
\sigma_{j}(p)=\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\bigl(\exp(te_{\alpha_{i_{j}}})\cdot p\bigr)
on
z_{k}
as a local function on
\mathcal{O}^{(\gamma_{j+1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell})}\subset Z_{(s_{i_{j+1}},\dots,s_{i_{\ell}})}
via the parametrisation

\mathbb{A}^{\ell-j}\ni(z_{j+1},\dots,z_{\ell})\mapsto\Phi_{(\gamma_{j+1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell})}(z_{j+1},\dots,z_{\ell}).

Remark 3.9
.

The formula (
7
) is calculated by considering the action of the bivector of the
j
-th factor on the rest of the variety. The first term in the formula arises from a torus component, and the second arises from a unipotent component. A recursive formula for
\sigma_{j}(z_{k})
is given in
[
10
, Theorem 4.10]
. In particular,
\sigma_{j}(z_{k})
is polynomial in the coordinates strictly between positions
j
and
k
, and at most quadratic in
z_{k}
.

Remark 3.10
.

When
\gamma_{j}<0
, its Bott–Samelson coordinate
z_{j}
is log-canonical with every Bott–Samelson coordinate to its right, but need not be so with the coordinates to its left. If we could somehow “move” this negative letter and its coordinate to a position to the left of all the others, it could then be log-canonical with every other coordinate.

Corollary 3.11
.

The isomorphism in Lemma
3.6
is a Poisson isomorphism.

Proof.

For
\mathcal{O}^{(\underline{w_{0}},\gamma)}\cap m^{-1}(w_{0}B)
, we observed that the coordinates
\zeta_{i}
for positions in
\underline{w}
are determined by the coordinates
z_{i}
for positions in
\gamma
. To verify the isomorphism is Poisson, it suffices to check that the bracket on the
z_{i}
’s agrees on both sides. This is immediate from Lemma
3.8
since the bracket
\{z_{j},z_{k}\}
depends only the signed expression in between positions
j
and
k
.
∎

Example 3.12
.

In type
A
, let
\underline{w}=(1,2,1,2)
and
\gamma=(1,2,1,-2)
. Denote coordinates for the Bott–Samelson charts

\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
by
(z_{1},\dots,z_{4})
and
(z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,z^{\prime}_{4})
respectively.
Calculating explicitly using Lemma
3.8
, we obtain

\begin{array}[]{l l l}\{z_{1},z_{2}\}_{\underline{w}}=-z_{1}z_{2},&\{z_{1},z_{3}\}_{\underline{w}}=z_{1}z_{3}-2z_{2},&\{z_{1},z_{4}\}_{\underline{w}}=2z_{1}z_{4}-2,\\
&\{z_{2},z_{3}\}_{\underline{w}}=-z_{2}z_{3},&\{z_{2},z_{4}\}_{\underline{w}}=z_{2}z_{4}-2z_{3},\\
&&\{z_{3},z_{4}\}_{\underline{w}}=-z_{3}z_{4}.\end{array}

and

\begin{array}[]{l l l}\{z^{\prime}_{1},z^{\prime}_{2}\}_{\gamma}=-z^{\prime}_{1}z^{\prime}_{2},&\{z^{\prime}_{1},z^{\prime}_{3}\}_{\gamma}=z^{\prime}_{1}z^{\prime}_{3}-2z^{\prime}_{2},&\{z^{\prime}_{1},z^{\prime}_{4}\}_{\gamma}=-2z^{\prime}_{1}z^{\prime}_{4}+(z^{\prime}_{4})^{2},\\
&\{z^{\prime}_{2},z^{\prime}_{3}\}_{\gamma}=-z^{\prime}_{2}z^{\prime}_{3},&\{z^{\prime}_{2},z^{\prime}_{4}\}_{\gamma}=-z^{\prime}_{2}z^{\prime}_{4}+2z^{\prime}_{3}(z^{\prime}_{4})^{2},\\
&&\{z^{\prime}_{3},z^{\prime}_{4}\}_{\gamma}=z^{\prime}_{3}z^{\prime}_{4}.\end{array}

Lemma 3.13
.

For any subexpression
\gamma
, the intersection
\mathcal{O}^{-\gamma}\cap\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
of two
opposite
Bott–Samelson charts in
Z_{\underline{w}}
is isomorphic to a torus
(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\ell}
. is a torus.

Proof.

We make use of the iterated
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration structure of Bott-Samelson varieties and proceed by induction on the length
\ell
. For
\ell=1
,
Z_{\underline{w}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and the intersection
\mathcal{O}^{(-i_{i})}\cap\mathcal{O}^{({i_{1}})}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,\infty\}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Let
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma[\ell-1]=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell-1})
denote the truncated subexpression of
\gamma
, and suppose that
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{O}^{-{{\gamma^{\prime}}}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\ell-1}
. The truncation map
\psi_{\underline{w}}:Z_{\underline{w}}\to Z_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration which can be trivialised over any of the Bott–Samelson charts. Restricting the domain to the chart
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
, we have a trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibration
\psi_{\underline{w}}:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}
for the two cases
\gamma=(\gamma^{\prime},-i_{\ell})
and
\gamma=(\gamma^{\prime},i_{\ell})
. Suppose we have
p\in Z_{\underline{w}}\setminus\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
such that
p^{\prime}=\psi_{\underline{w}}(p)\in\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{O}^{{-\gamma^{\prime}}}
.

In the first case, when
\gamma_{\ell}={i_{\ell}}
, trivialising in the coordinates for
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}
gives

p=\Phi_{(\gamma^{\prime},-i_{\ell})}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell-1},0)=\bigl[\Phi_{\gamma^{\prime}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell-1}),e\bigr].

Note that this is gives section of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration

\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{O}^{(\gamma^{\prime},-i_{\ell})}\subset\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\quad p^{\prime}=\Phi_{\gamma^{\prime}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell-1})\mapsto\bigr[\Phi_{\gamma^{\prime}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell-1}),e\bigl].

Since
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{O}^{-{\gamma^{\prime}}}
, we can re-trivialise in of coordinates for
\mathcal{O}^{-\gamma^{\prime}}
,

p=\Phi_{(-\gamma^{\prime},-i_{\ell})}\bigl(\zeta_{1}(z_{1}),\zeta_{2}(z_{1},z_{2}),\dots,\zeta_{\ell-1}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell-1}),0\bigr)\in\mathcal{O}^{-{\gamma}}

to show that
p\in\mathcal{O}^{-\gamma}
.
Here, the
\zeta_{i}
denote the change of coordinate functions between
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{O}^{-{\gamma^{\prime}}}
. The last coordinate can be seen to remain as zero since the
B
-action arising from the
\zeta
changes will commute past the identity element.

In the other case, we have
\gamma_{\ell}=-i_{\ell}
. Again trivialising in the coordinates for
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}
, our condition
p\notin\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
implies we must have

p=\Phi_{(\gamma^{\prime},i_{\ell})}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell-1},0)=\bigl[\Phi_{\gamma^{\prime}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell-1}),\dot{s}_{i_{\ell}}\bigr].

Again, we note that this gives a section of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration

\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{O}^{(\gamma^{\prime},i_{\ell})}\subset\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\quad p^{\prime}=\Phi_{\gamma^{\prime}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell-1})\mapsto\bigl[\Phi_{\gamma^{\prime}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell-1}),\dot{s_{i_{\ell}}}\bigr].

Since,
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{O}^{-{\gamma^{\prime}}}
, we can re-trivialise
p
in terms of coordinates for
\mathcal{O}^{-{\gamma^{\prime}}}
, to get

p=\Phi_{-{\gamma}}\left(\zeta_{1}(z_{1}),\zeta_{2}(z_{1},z_{2}),\dots,\zeta_{\ell-1}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell-1}),\zeta_{\ell}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell-1})\right)\in\mathcal{O}^{-{\gamma}}

where the
\zeta_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq\ell-1
denotes the change of coordinate functions between
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{O}^{-\gamma^{\prime}}
, and
\zeta_{\ell}
is the appropriate coordinate change arising from moving the Borel action rightwards.
In general it will be the parameter arising from the one parameter subgroup associated to the root
i_{\ell}
up to a Laurent monomial in the nonzero functions defining the
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{O}^{-\gamma^{\prime}}
.

Thus, the charts
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
and
\mathcal{O}^{-\gamma}
are fibrewise distinct
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibrations which cover the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration, and whose complements are non-intersecting sections in a trivial
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration over the torus
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{O}^{-\gamma^{\prime}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\ell-1}
. They must intersect to a trivial
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-fibration over
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\ell-1}
. Thus,
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\cap\mathcal{O}^{-{\gamma}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\ell}
.
∎

Most notably, the formula (
7
) says the Bott–Samelson coordinates for the “unsupported chart”
\mathcal{O}^{-\underline{w}}=\mathcal{O}^{(-i_{1},,-i_{2},\dots,-i_{\ell})}
are automatically log-canonical, and therefore the torus
\{z_{j}\neq 0\}
is a log-canonical torus.

Corollary 3.14
.

The torus
\mathcal{O}^{-\underline{w}}\cap\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
is given by the complement of the coordinate hyperplanes in
\mathcal{O}^{-\underline{w}}
, and is hence a log-canonical torus in
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
.

Remark 3.15
.

This is the torus corresponding to the seed
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
, see Lemma
4.15
.
This gives a quick verification that the initial clusters of
[
34
,
6
,
18
]
in
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
are log-canonical with the standard Poisson structure.
Furthermore, in
\mathcal{O}^{-\underline{w}}
, since this torus is the complement of the coordinate hyperplanes, if we take each
\zeta_{i}
to be frozen, then we trivially have
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{U}=\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{-\underline{w}}]
, for an initial seed comprised solely of noninvertible frozens.

We can determine the complementary hypersurfaces for this torus in
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
by finding the change of coordinates from
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
to
\mathcal{O}^{-\underline{w}}
. To do this, we iteratively invert the leftmost coordinate and modify the other functions accordingly. This rational map factors as a composition of
\ell
changes of coordinates through adjacent Bott–Samelson charts

\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}=\mathcal{O}^{(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell})}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(-i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell})}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(-i_{1},-i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell})}\dashrightarrow\cdots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{-\underline{w}}.

This idea of finding rational maps which are defined on a log-canonical torus is the basis of our interpretation of Demazure weaves, and we expanded upon in later sections.

4.
Demazure weaves, framed weaves, and opening charts

In this section we recall the key constructions of Demazure weaves from
[
6
]
, and verify their compatibility with the standard Poisson structure.

4.1.
Demazure weaves

A
Demazure weave

[
6
, §4.1]
is a planar graph consisting of
I
-coloured edges and vertices of specific valences arising from the Dynkin diagram and computations with braid matrices. Figure
1
below is recreated from
[
6
]
for the purposes of comparison with the next section.

Figure 1
.
The three types of vertices in classical Demazure weaves of simply-laced type. We keep to the convention in
[
6
]
of using
blue
,
red
, and
green
to denote edges labelled
i,j,k
where
i
and
j
are adjacent roots, and
i
and
k
are not adjacent.

Each (generic) horizontal slice of a Demazure weave is a word with letters in
I
. Demazure weaves diagrammatically capture the data of a sequence of rational maps between Bott–Samelson cells. In
[
6
]
, the hexavalent and tetravalent vertices correspond to biregular isomorphisms between cells arising from applying braid moves to the words. The trivalent vertices or “pinches” correspond to rational maps defined on the non-vanishing of a particular rational function.
In this way, each strand carries a rational function in the initial Bott–Samelson coordinates
z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell}
. The dotted ray propagating rightward from the trivalent vertex is called a
raking ray
(see
[
6
, Definition 5.1]
), and is used to keep track of how to modify each variable to the right
1
1

1

The conventions for Demazure weaves in
[
7
]
and
[
6
]
differ by a horizontal flip and transposing. We follow the latter, as it matches the construction of Bott–Samelson charts in
[
10
]
.
of a trivalent vertex.

The respective matrix identities for the three vertices in Figure
1
are

(8)

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})B_{j}(z_{2})B_{i}(z_{3})

\displaystyle=B_{j}(z_{3})B_{i}(z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2})B_{j}(z_{1}),

(9)

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})B_{k}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=B_{k}(z_{2})B_{i}(z_{1}),\text{ and}

(10)

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})B_{i}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=B_{i}(z_{1}-z_{2}^{-1})\alpha^{\vee}_{i}(z_{2})u_{i}(-z_{2}^{-1}).

Here the element
\alpha^{\vee}_{i}(z_{2})u_{i}(-z_{2}^{-1})
of the Borel subgroup can propagate rightwards and modify the other coordinates appropriately.

Remark 4.1
.

In non-simply-laced type, higher valency vertices are needed. These correspond to relations between roots of a
B_{2}
or
G_{2}
sub-root system. See
[
6
, §6.1]
.

It is shown that over a whole weave, each pinch only adds one new irreducible component to the indeterminacy locus of the overall composed map. The other zeroes or poles were previously acquired on previous pinches, and so these can be collected to obtain a toric chart on the braid variety (or on Bott–Samelson cells).

Weaves can be interpreted as giving conditions for a moduli of flags. Given a weave
\mathfrak{w}
we can label each region in the plane with a flag
\mathcal{B}
such that whenever two flags are separated by an
i
-coloured strand, they are in relative position
s_{i}
. If
g\cdot B
is the flag to the left of an
i
-coloured strand with variable
z
, then we want the flag on the right of the strand to be equal to
gB_{i}(z)\cdot B
. In this sense, the flags along top-most slice of a Demazure weave correspond to the open condition of the Bott–Samelson cell. After the variables at the bottom-most strands are declared to be zero, the flags along the bottom-most slice of a weave contribute the closed condition for the braid variety: that the left-most and right-most flags differ by exactly
\delta(\beta)
. The subset
M(\mathfrak{w})
of the braid variety where the interior regions can be filled by flags will be a cluster torus.

Remark 4.2
.

For open Richardson varieties
\mathring{Z}^{v}_{\underline{w}}=\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}\cap m^{-1}(B^{-}vB)
, we would need to impose the condition that the last flag is in the
B^{-}
orbit of
vB
, but this construction of weaves only allows us to impose a condition of being in the fibre
m^{-1}(vB/B)
of torus fixed points. These coincide for the longest element
w_{0}
, but not for other elements
v
. This was one inspiration for the “negative strands” of the later sections.

4.2.
Toric charts and mutations of weaves

There is a collection of equivalence relations between weaves, which can be found in
[
6
, §4.2]
. The key property of weave equivalences
\mathfrak{w}_{1}\sim\mathfrak{w}_{2}
are that the two composed maps between Bott–Samelson cells map are equal and are in particular defined on the same open torus. It is checked in
[
7
, §5]
that two equivalent weaves give the same tori.

Lemma 4.3

(
[
6
, Lemma 4.4]
)
.

Fix a word for
\delta(\underline{w})
. Any two weaves
\mathfrak{w}_{1},\mathfrak{w}_{2}:\underline{w}\to\delta(\underline{w})
are related by a sequence of weave equivalences and mutations.

4.3.
Opening sequences of crossings

Some of the ideas in this work appeared previously in
[
7
]
, but our interpretations using Bott–Samelson charts are new. In
[
7
, §2.3]
, a number of toric charts are constructed on braid varieties based on “opening the crossings” in the braid word. This can be thought of as a sequence of maps between Bott–Samelson cells where we remove one letter at a time (in some order), and the complement of the indeterminacy locus gives the torus. Different orderings might yield the same or different toric charts. In
[
7
, §5.3]
, it is shown that these toric charts obtained by can be realised as the complements of the indeterminacy loci of Demazure weaves.

One way to think of a sequence of opening crossings (or its associated Demazure weaves) is to consider it an oriented
\ell
-dim hypercube
[
7
, Figure 9]
, with vertices labelled by the subexpressions
\gamma\in\Upsilon_{\underline{w}}
and edges between adjacent subexpressions oriented
(\gamma^{\prime},i_{j},\gamma^{\prime\prime})\to(\gamma^{\prime},-i_{j},\gamma^{\prime\prime})
. Travelling along an edge corresponds to opening the crossing at that position, and there are
\ell!
of these paths from the vertex
\underline{w}
to
-\underline{w}
.
This hypercube is closely related to the graph of adjacent Bott–Samelson charts.

4.4.
Quivers from weaves

A procedure is given in
[
6
]
to construct a quiver from a particular class of cycles on weaves called
Lusztig cycles
. Constructing these cycles requires a more detailed labelling on the weave and instead corresponds to a moduli of framed flags.

Elements of the basic affine space
G/U
are called
framed flags
in
[
6
]
,
decorated flags
in
[
34
]
, and
weighted flags
in
[
18
]
. There is a natural projection
G/U\to G/B
whose fibres are isomorphic to the torus
T=B/U
. If a flag is an equivalence class of an ordered sequence of linearly independent vectors, lifting this flag to
G/U
is a choice of the lengths of these vectors (i.e. basis of these one dim subspaces).

Lemma 4.4

(
[
6
, Proposition 3.11]
)
.

For
g\cdot U\in G/U
a framed flag and parameters
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
, and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, if
gB_{i}(z)\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x)\cdot U=gB_{i}(z^{\prime})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x^{\prime})\cdot U
, then
z=z^{\prime}
and
x=x^{\prime}
.

In
[
34
]
, flags of of the above form where
x=1
are called
compatible
.
Following
[
6
, Definitons 5.3, 5.8]
, a
framed labelling

(\mathfrak{w},\zeta)
is an assignment of two rational functions
(z,x)
are assigned to each edge on the weave
\mathfrak{w}
, so that an
i
-coloured strand separates two framed flags
g\cdot U
and
gB_{i}(z)\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x)\cdot U
as described by the lemma above. Labels propagate down the weave in a manner consistent with the relations (or their framed counterparts) represented by the vertices of the weave. At hexavalent vertices, there is a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
choice of how to propagate the torus components, and a tropicalisation of braid identities in Lusztig coordinates is used to determine the propagation.

For any trivalent vertex
v\in\mathfrak{w}
, there is a unique Lusztig cycle
\gamma_{v}:E(\mathfrak{w})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
which originates from
v
. This is defined by propagation rules of
Lusztig cycles
defined in
[
6
, Definition 4.9]
(see also
[
5
, Definition 4.17]
).

Theorem 4.5

(
[
6
, Theorem 5.12]
)
.

For a weave
\mathfrak{w}
and
\mathfrak{w}_{3}
its set of trivalent vertices, there is a unique collection of rational functions
\{A_{v}\}_{v\in\mathfrak{w}_{3}}
and a unique framed labelling
(\mathfrak{w},\zeta)
of the weave such that the torus label
x_{e}
of every edge
e
is given by

x_{e}=\prod_{v\in\mathfrak{w}_{3}}A_{v}^{\gamma_{v}({e})},

where
\gamma_{v}
is the Lusztig cycle originating from
v
.

The quiver from a weave is defined by adding up local contributions arising from each vertex, together with a contribution from the bottom boundary of the weave. These have a topological interpretation where these are intersection numbers of particular cycles on surfaces, see
[
6
, § 7.4]
.
From
[
6
, §6.1]
, the intersections between Lusztig cycles (and dual Lusztig cycles) at a slice
\gamma
of length
r
in a weave is given by

\sharp_{\gamma}(C^{\vee}\cdot C^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,k=1}^{r}\mathrm{sign}(j-k)c_{j}^{\vee}c_{k}\cdot\langle\gamma^{j}(\alpha_{i_{j}}),\gamma^{k}(\alpha_{i_{k}})\rangle.

It’s shown in
[
6
, Corollary 4.45]
that the quiver arising from this intersection form coincides with the quiver in
[
34
]
for the open part of the Bott–Samelson cell
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}\cap m^{-1}(B^{-}B/B)
. The coincidence of the cluster variables is shown in
[
6
, Proposition 5.20]
.

4.5.
Weave tori are log-canonical in the standard Poisson structure

Equipping the Bott–Samelson cells with the standard Poisson structure, we have the following results.

Proposition 4.6
.

The maps between
2
and
3
dimensional Bott–Samelson cells corresponding to tetravalent and hexavalent vertices are Poisson isomorphisms.

Proof.

For non-adjacent roots
i
and
k
, the map
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}=\mathcal{O}^{(i,k)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(k,i)}=\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}
is given by
\Phi_{\gamma}(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto\Phi_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(z_{2},z_{1})
. By the Elek–Lu formula in Lemma
3.8
, the bracket between the coordinate functions is trivial on both the domain and codomain of this isomorphism, and so the map
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}
is a Poisson isomorphism.

Now let
i
and
j
be adjacent roots and consider the map
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}=\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(j,i,j)}=\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}
defined by
\Phi_{\gamma}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto\Phi_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(z_{3},z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2},z_{1})
.
Using Lemma
3.8
, the Poisson bracket on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
in the Bott–Samelson coordinates is

\{z_{1},z_{2}\}_{\gamma}=-z_{1}z_{2},\quad\{z_{1},z_{3}\}_{\gamma}=z_{1}z_{3}-2z_{2},\quad\{z_{2},z_{3}\}_{\gamma}=-z_{2}z_{3},

and the exact same for the coordinates
\widetilde{z}_{i}
on
\mathcal{O}^{(j,i,j)}
.
Under the change of coordinates

f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{1})=z_{3},\quad f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{2})=z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2},\quad f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{3})=z_{1},

we have

\displaystyle f^{*}\left(\{\widetilde{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{2}\}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}\right)

\displaystyle=f^{*}\left(-\widetilde{z}_{1}\widetilde{z}_{2}\right)=-z_{3}(z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}),

\displaystyle=\{z_{3},z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}\}_{\gamma}=\{f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{1}),f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{2})\}_{\gamma},

\displaystyle f^{*}\left(\{\widetilde{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{3}\}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}\right)

\displaystyle=f^{*}\left(\widetilde{z}_{1}\widetilde{z}_{3}-2\widetilde{z}_{2}\right)=-(z_{1}z_{3}-2z_{2}),

\displaystyle=\{z_{3},z_{1}\}_{\gamma}=\{f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{1}),f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{3})\}_{\gamma},\qquad\text{ and}

\displaystyle f^{*}\left(\{\widetilde{z}_{2},\widetilde{z}_{3}\}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}\right)

\displaystyle=f^{*}\left(-\widetilde{z}_{2}\widetilde{z}_{3}\right)=-z_{1}(z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}),

\displaystyle=\{z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2},z_{1}\}_{\gamma}=\{f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{2}),f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{3})\}_{\gamma}.

Hence the map
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}
given by
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto(z_{3},z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2},z_{1})
is a Poisson map.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

The above isomorphisms are a result of being these Bott–Samelson cells being Poisson isomorphic to the Schubert cell. When
i
and
j
are adjacent roots, both
\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i)}
and
\mathcal{O}^{(j,i,j)}
are Poisson isomorphic to the Schubert cell
Bs_{i}s_{j}s_{i}B/B
, and the coordinate change is simply from comparing the parametrisations. Similarly, when
i,k
are not adjacent, both
\mathcal{O}^{(i,k)}
and
\mathcal{O}^{(k,i)}
are Poisson isomorphic to
Bs_{i}s_{k}B/B
.

We’ve checked that these give Poisson isomorphisms between
2
and
3
dimensional Bott–Samelson cells respectively. Now we show that these isomorphisms can be applied to a consecutive string within a word.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
X,Y,Z
be Poisson varieties,
\varphi:X\to Y
a surjective Poisson regular map, and
\psi:Y\to Z
a regular map such that the composition
\psi\circ\varphi:X\to Y
is Poisson. Then
\psi
is Poisson.

Proof.

For rational functions
f,g
on
Z
, we have

\{f,g\}_{Z}\circ(\psi\circ\varphi)=\{f\circ(\psi\circ\varphi),g\circ(\psi\circ\varphi)\}_{X}=\{f\circ\psi,g\circ\psi\}_{Y}\circ\varphi.

We conclude that
\{f,g\}_{Z}\circ\psi=\{f\circ\psi,g\circ\psi\}_{Y}
as
\varphi
is surjective.
∎

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
m:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to G/B
and
\widetilde{m}:\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}\to G/B
denote the multiplication maps from two Bott–Samelson cells. A Poisson regular map
f:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}
which satisfies
\widetilde{m}\circ f=m

induces Poisson regular maps between higher dimensional Bott–Samelson cells

\mathcal{O}^{(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime})}\to\mathcal{O}^{(\gamma^{\prime},\widetilde{\gamma},\gamma^{\prime\prime})}.

Proof.

For
\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell})
, let
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\cdot B
denote the right
B
-orbit of the image of the map
\Phi_{\gamma}
in
P_{\gamma_{1}}\times^{B}\cdots\times^{B}P_{\gamma_{\ell}}
, (note the lack of a quotient by
B
on the rightmost factor).

The Bott–Samelson cells can be trivialised by their intermediary products, by which we mean
\mathcal{O}^{(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime})}
is (Poisson) isomorphic to
(\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\cdot B)\times^{B}(\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\cdot B)\times^{B}\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}
. Suppose we have Poisson regular map
f:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}
between Bott–Samelson charts. Then we have a natural map between the product spaces given by taking the identity on the other two factors. That the map
f
respects the multiplication maps gives that
\mathrm{id}\times f\times\mathrm{id}
map is
B^{2}
-equivariant and hence descends to a map between the quotient spaces. We have the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\cdot B)\times(\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\cdot B)\times\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\cdot B)\times(\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}\cdot B)\times\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\times^{B}\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\times^{B}\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime}}\times^{B}\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}\times^{B}\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\times f\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}

Note that
\widetilde{q}\circ(\mathrm{id}\times f\times\mathrm{id})
is a Poisson map as a composition of two Poisson maps, and that
q
is a surjective Poisson map. Applying Lemma
4.8
, we conclude that the induced map
\widetilde{f}
between the Bott–Samelson cells is Poisson.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\underline{w}
be a word with
i_{j-1}=i_{j}
, and denote by
\tilde{\underline{w}}
the word of length
\ell-1
obtained by omitting the
j
-th letter. There is a canonical Poisson map
m_{j}:Z_{\underline{w}}\to Z_{\tilde{\underline{w}}}
obtained by combining the
(j-1)
and
j
-th entries,

[g_{1},\dots,g_{j-2},g_{j-1},g_{j},g_{j+1},\dots,g_{\ell}]\mapsto[g_{1},\dots,g_{j-2},g_{j-1}g_{j},g_{j+1},\dots,g_{\ell}].

Proof.

Let
\underline{w}=(\underline{w^{\prime}},i,i,\underline{w^{\prime\prime}})
. Applying the multiplication map to the two
P_{i}
factors in

P_{\underline{w^{\prime}}}\times P_{i}\times P_{i}\times P_{\underline{w^{\prime\prime}}}\to P_{\underline{w^{\prime}}}\times P_{i}\times P_{\underline{w^{\prime\prime}}}

defines a
B^{\ell}
-equivariant map, and hence descends to a map on Bott–Samelson varieties
m_{j}:Z_{\underline{w}}\to Z_{\tilde{\underline{w}}}
. That this map is Poisson is an application of Lemma
4.8
.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

If we consider the Bott–Samelson varieties using their embeddings as sequences of flags, this map corresponds to forgetting the
(j-1)
-th flag in the sequence.

To show that trivalent vertices respect the standard Poisson structure, it is helpful think of the rational map between Bott–Samelson cells as being first factored through an adjacent Bott–Samelson chart:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{(\gamma^{\prime},i,-i,\gamma^{\prime\prime})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{(\gamma^{\prime},i,i,\gamma^{\prime\prime})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{(\gamma^{\prime},i,\gamma^{\prime\prime})}}}

Here, the first map is a change of coordinates within the same Poisson variety
Z_{\underline{w}}
and is hence Poisson.
It is of the form

\Phi_{(\gamma^{\prime},i,i,\gamma^{\prime\prime})}(z_{1},\dots,z_{j-1},z_{j},z_{j+1}),\dots,z_{\ell})\mapsto\Phi_{(\gamma^{\prime},i,-i,\gamma^{\prime\prime})}(z_{1},\dots,z_{j-1},z_{j}^{-1},z_{j+1}^{\prime}),\dots,z_{\ell}^{\prime})

where
z_{j+1}^{\prime},\cdots,z_{\ell}^{\prime}
are rational functions possibly with poles given by powers of
z_{j}
.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\underline{w}
be a word with
i_{j-1}=i_{j}=i
, and let
\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{j-1},\gamma_{j},\dots,\gamma_{\ell})
be a subexpression of length
\ell
with
\gamma_{j-1}=i_{j-1}
and
\gamma_{j}=-i_{j}=-i_{j-1}
. Let
\underline{\tilde{w}}
and
\tilde{\gamma}
be obtained by omitting the
j
-th entry in both of the above.
Then we have a biregular isomorphism
f:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\tilde{\gamma}}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}

given by

(z_{1},\dots,z_{j-1},z_{j},\dots,z_{n})\mapsto\bigl((z_{1},\dots,z_{j-2},z_{j-1}-z_{j},z_{j+1},\dots,z_{\ell}),z_{j}\bigr).

Furthermore, if
\gamma_{k}>0
implies
\gamma_{k^{\prime}}>0
for all
k<k^{\prime}<j
, then we can endow the codomain with a log-canonical bracket on the product space to make this isomorphism a Poisson map.

Proof.

The map onto the first factor is a result of writing the Poisson map in Lemma
4.10
in the Bott–Samelson coordinates for these charts. Alternatively, a case-by-case argument could check the bracket in Lemma
3.8
on each of the coordinate functions.

The log-canonicity of the coordinate
z_{j}
in the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
factor with the other coordinate functions arises from the Elek–Lu formula (
7
). In the domain,
z_{j}
is log-canonical with
z_{k}
for
k>j
since the
j
-th letter is unsupported. For the log-canonicity of
z_{j-1}-z_{j}
with
z_{j}
, the Elek–Lu formula gives that
\{z_{j-1},z_{j}\}=-\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle z_{j-1}z_{j}+\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle z_{j}^{2}
, and therefore

\{z_{j-1}-z_{j},z_{j}\}=-\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle(z_{j-1}-z_{j})z_{j}.

Finally, for
k<(j-1)
, if
\gamma_{k}
is negative, then log-canonicity of
z_{k}
is immediate from (
7
). If
\gamma_{k}
is positive, then our assumption on the positivity of the letters between
k
and
j
is positive means that by
[
10
, Lemma 4.6]
the vector field acting on
z_{j-1}
has no torus component, and therefore
\sigma_{\alpha_{i_{j}}}(z_{j})=0
by
[
10
, Lemma 4.6 (1)]
.
∎

The above discussion verifies that the map in
[
7
, Lemma 2.22]
is Poisson. Together with Proposition
4.6
, each component map of a Demazure weave is Poisson.

Theorem 4.13
.

Each toric chart
T_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset X(\underline{w})
on braid varieties obtained from a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}:(\underline{w})\to\delta(\underline{w})
are log-canonical with respect to the standard Poisson structure.

Proof.

The torus associated to a weave is obtained by iteratively factoring out a torus factor from trivalent vertices. Our positivity assumption on the subexpression
\gamma
in Lemma
4.12
applies to Bott–Samelson cells, and therefore we have the log-canonicity of this torus factor the other coordinates at each step. Proposition
4.6
gives that the isomorphisms coming from tetravalent and hexavalent vertices are indeed Poisson isomorphisms, and therefore the log-canonicity is preserved at each stage between the trivalent vertices.
∎

4.6.
Compatibility of the cluster and Poisson structures

The previous subsection showed that every weave gives a log-canonical torus. However, for quivers of infinite mutation type, there can be seeds which are not represented by any weave. In this section, we use Lemma
2.11
and check that all such cluster tori are log-canonical.

Remark 4.14
.

The statements in this subsection for the Bott–Samelson cell are known since the Bott–Samelson coordinates give a Poisson CGL presentation
[
10
, Theorem 5.12]
on the coordinate ring of
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
. The coincidence of the initial seed
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
with the initial seed from Poisson CGL theory
[
25
]
has been checked in ongoing work by Lu and Mi
[
28
]
. For completeness, we provide a self-contained check of compatibility without using Poisson CGL theory.

Recall the successor and predecessor functions from (
3
) and (
4
). Define two
\ell\times\ell
upper triangular integer matrices
\Lambda=\Lambda(\underline{w})=(\lambda_{jk})
and
Q=Q(\underline{w})=(q_{jk})
whose entries are respectively given by

\lambda_{jk}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }k=j,\\
-1&\text{if }k=s(j),\\
0&\text{else},\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad q_{jk}=\begin{cases}-1&\text{if }k=j,\\
-a_{i_{k},i_{j}}&\text{if }k>j,\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\zeta_{i}
denote the Bott–Samelson coordinates on
\mathcal{O}^{-\underline{w}}
. For the regular functions
\varphi_{j}=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{j}},\omega_{i_{j}}}\circ m\circ\psi_{\underline{w},j}
on
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
, we have a monomial change of coordinates

\underline{\zeta}=\underline{\varphi}^{\Lambda Q}.

on the intersection
\mathcal{O}^{-\underline{w}}\cap\mathcal{O}^{-\underline{w}}
.

Proof.

Let
C=\Lambda Q=(c_{jk})
. The claim is that for each
1\leq j\leq\ell
, we have
\zeta_{k}=\varphi_{1}^{c_{1k}}\varphi_{2}^{c_{2k}}\cdots\varphi_{k}^{c_{kk}}
where

(11)

c_{jk}=\begin{cases}-1&\text{if }j=k,\\
-a_{i_{k},i_{j}}&\text{if }j<k<s(j),\\
-2-(-1)&\text{if }s(j)=k,\\
-a_{i_{k},i_{j}}-(-a_{i_{k},i_{s(j)}})&\text{if }s(j)<k,\\
0&\text{else},\end{cases}=\begin{cases}-1&\text{if }j=k,\\
-a_{i_{k},i_{j}}&\text{if }j<k<s(j),\\
-1&\text{if }k=s(j),\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

We use induction on
k
.
When
k=1
, the identity
B_{i}(z)=u_{-i}(z^{-1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(z)u_{i}(-z^{-1})
for the root
i=i_{k}
gives that
\zeta=\frac{1}{\varphi_{1}}
as required.
When
p(k)=-\infty
we have,

\displaystyle\varphi_{k}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{k}},\omega_{i_{k}}}(B_{\underline{w}[k]}(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})),

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{k}},\omega_{i_{k}}}\left(B_{\underline{w}[k-1]}(z_{1},\dots,z_{k-1})\cdot B_{i_{k}}(z_{k})\right),

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{k}},\omega_{i_{k}}}\left(B_{i_{k}}(z_{k})\right),

\displaystyle=z_{k}.

And therefore,
For
k>1
, with
p(k)\neq-\infty
, let
j_{1},\dots,j_{m}
be the last instances of each letter in
\underline{w}[k-1]
. Then we have,

\displaystyle\varphi_{k}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{k}},\omega_{i_{k}}}(B_{\underline{w}[k]}(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})),

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{k}},\omega_{i_{k}}}\left(B_{\underline{w}[k-1]}(z_{1},\dots,z_{k-1})\cdot B_{i_{k}}(z_{k})\right),

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{k}},\omega_{i_{k}}}\left(\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}u_{-i_{j}}(\zeta_{j})\right)\left(\prod_{j<k\,:\;s(j)\geq k}\alpha_{i_{j}}^{\vee}(\varphi_{j})\right)U\cdot B_{i_{k}}(z_{k})\right),

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{k}},\omega_{i_{k}}}\left(\alpha_{i_{k}}^{\vee}(\varphi_{p(k)})B_{i_{k}}(z_{k}^{\prime})\right),

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{k}},\omega_{i_{k}}}\left(\alpha_{i_{k}}^{\vee}(\varphi_{p(k)})u_{-i_{k}}((z_{k}^{\prime})^{-1})\alpha_{i_{k}}(z_{k}^{\prime})u_{i_{k}}(-(z_{k}^{\prime})^{-1})\right),

\displaystyle=\varphi_{p(k)}z_{k}^{\prime},

where
z_{k}^{\prime}=z_{k}^{\prime}(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})
is the polynomial resulting from moving the unipotent matrix U rightward. Commuting matrices, we have the matrix identities

\displaystyle B_{\underline{w}[k]}(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})

\displaystyle=B_{\underline{w}[k-1]}(z_{1},\dots,z_{k-1})\cdot B_{i_{k}}(z_{k}),

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}u_{-i_{j}}(\zeta_{j})\right)\left(\prod_{j<k\,:\;s(j)\geq k}\alpha_{i_{j}}^{\vee}(\varphi_{j})\right)B_{i_{k}}(z_{k}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}u_{-i_{j}}(\zeta_{j})\right)\left(\prod_{j<k\,:\;s(j)\geq k}\alpha_{i_{j}}^{\vee}(\varphi_{j})\right)u_{-i_{k}}((z_{k}^{\prime})^{-1})\alpha_{i_{k}}(z_{k}^{\prime})u_{i_{k}}(-z_{k}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}u_{-i_{j}}(\zeta_{j})\right)u_{-i_{k}}\left((z_{k}^{\prime})^{-1}\left(\prod_{j<k\,:\;s(j)\geq k}\varphi_{j}^{-a_{i_{k},i_{j}}}\right)\right)b

for appropriate Borel element
b\in B
.
From this, we read

\displaystyle\zeta_{k}

\displaystyle=(z_{k}^{\prime})^{-1}\left(\prod_{j<k\,:\;s(j)\geq k}\varphi_{j}^{-a_{i_{k},i_{j}}}\right)=(z_{k}^{\prime})^{-1}\varphi_{p(k)}^{-2}\left(\prod_{j<k\,:\;s(j)>k}\varphi_{j}^{-a_{i_{k},i_{j}}}\right),

\displaystyle=\varphi_{p(k)}^{-1}\varphi_{k}^{-1}\left(\prod_{j<k\,:\;s(j)>k}\varphi_{j}^{-a_{i_{k},i_{j}}}\right).\qed

We now have an explicit description of initial cluster variables
A_{j}=\varphi_{j}
of the Bott–Samelson cell
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
as Laurent monomials in the coordinates
\zeta_{j}
of the opposite chart
\mathcal{O}^{-\underline{w}}
. The Elek–Lu formula (
7
