) gives that these are log-canonical, and therefore the
A_{j}
are log-canonical. The following statement allows us to determine the Poisson coefficient matrix for the cluster variables of the cell
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
\underline{\zeta}=(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{\ell})
and
\underline{\varphi}=(\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{\ell})
be two collections of log-canonical coordinates with Poisson coefficient matrices
\varTheta=(\vartheta_{ij})
and
\Omega=(\omega_{ij})
so that

\{\zeta_{i},\zeta_{j}\}=\vartheta_{ij}\zeta_{i}\zeta_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\{\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}\}=\omega_{ij}\varphi_{i}\varphi_{j}.

If
\underline{\zeta}=\underline{\varphi}^{C}
for an integer matrix
C=(c_{ij})
, then we have
\varTheta=C^{T}\Omega C
.

Proof.

We calculate:

\displaystyle\vartheta_{ij}\zeta_{i}\zeta_{j}

\displaystyle=\{\zeta_{i},\zeta_{j}\}=\left\{\prod_{k=1}^{\ell}\varphi_{k}^{c_{ki}},\zeta_{j}\right\},

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}c_{ki}\frac{\varphi_{1}^{c_{1i}}\cdots\varphi_{\ell}^{c_{\ell i}}}{\varphi_{k}}\{\varphi_{k},\zeta_{j}\},

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}c_{ki}\frac{\varphi_{1}^{c_{1i}}\cdots\varphi_{\ell}^{c_{\ell i}}}{\varphi_{k}}\left(\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}c_{k^{\prime}j}\frac{\varphi_{1}^{c_{1j}}\cdots\varphi_{\ell}^{c_{\ell j}}}{\varphi_{k^{\prime}}}\{\varphi_{k},\varphi_{k^{\prime}}\}\right),

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}c_{ki}(\varphi_{1}^{c_{1i}}\cdots\varphi_{\ell}^{c_{\ell i}})\left(\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}c_{k^{\prime}j}(\varphi_{1}^{c_{1j}}\cdots\varphi_{\ell}^{c_{\ell j}})\omega_{kk^{\prime}}\right),

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{k,k^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}c_{ki}\omega_{kk^{\prime}}c_{k^{\prime}j}\right)\zeta_{i}\zeta_{j},

Therefore,
\vartheta_{ij}=\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{\ell}\left(\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}c_{ki}\omega_{kk^{\prime}}\right)c_{k^{\prime}j}\right)
, and
\varTheta=C^{T}\Omega C
.
∎

From (
7
), we know that the Poisson coefficient matrix of
(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{\ell})
has entries
a_{jk}=\langle\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}\rangle
for
j<k
, and hence
\varTheta=(\varDelta Q)^{T}-\varDelta Q
where
\varDelta=\mathrm{diag}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{\ell})
is a diagonal matrix whose entries

\delta_{j}=d_{i_{j}}

are from the symmetriser
D=\mathrm{diag}(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
of the Cartan matrix. Therefore, the Poisson coefficient matrix
\Omega
for the seed
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
is

(12)

\begin{split}\Omega=(C^{T})^{-1}\varTheta C^{-1}&=\left((\Lambda Q)^{T}\right)^{-1}\left((\varDelta Q)^{T}-\varDelta Q\right)(\Lambda Q)^{-1},\\
&=(\Lambda^{T})^{-1}\left(\varDelta Q^{-1}-(Q^{T})^{-1}\varDelta\right)\Lambda^{-1}.\end{split}

Lemma 4.17
.

The submatrix
\widetilde{E}
of
E(\underline{w})
formed by the mutable columns is equal to the mutable columns of the product
\Lambda Q\Lambda^{T}
.

Proof.

Again, let
C=\Lambda Q
. Whenever
s(k)<\infty
, we have
(C\Lambda^{T})_{j,k}=c_{j,k}-c_{j,s(k)}
.
We can then check case-by-case that using the description of
C
from (
11
), that

(\Lambda Q\Lambda^{T})_{j,k}=(C\Lambda^{T})_{j,k}=\begin{cases}a_{i_{k},i_{j}}&\text{if }k<j<s(k)\leq s(j),\\
-a_{i_{k},i_{j}}&\text{if }j<k<s(j)\leq s(k),\\
1&\text{if }j=s(k),\\
-1&\text{if }j=p(k),\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}

whenever
k
is a mutable index, i.e.
s(k)<\infty
. This matches the description of the exchange matrix from (
5
).
∎

Lemma 4.18
.

We have
\left[I_{\mathrm{uf}}\;\;0\right](\varDelta^{-1}\Lambda Q^{T}\varDelta)=-\left[I_{\mathrm{uf}}\;\;0\right](\Lambda Q)
.

Proof.

We need to show that the two matrices
\varDelta^{-1}\Lambda Q^{T}\varDelta
and
-\Lambda Q
agree on their mutable rows.
Note that when
s(j)<\infty
, we have
(\Lambda Q^{T})_{j,k}=q_{k,j}-q_{k,s(j)}
. Hence,

(\Lambda Q^{T})_{j,k}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }j=k,\\
a_{i_{j},i_{k}}&\text{if }j<k<s(j),\\
1&\text{if }j=s(k),\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}

We have
(\varDelta^{-1}\Lambda Q^{T}\varDelta)_{j,k}=\frac{\delta_{k}}{\delta_{j}}\cdot(\Lambda Q^{T})_{j,k}
. Since
\varDelta
was defined in terms of the symmetrisers of the Cartan matrix,
a_{i_{j},i_{k}}\delta_{k}=a_{i_{k},i_{j}}\delta_{j}
, and we are done after comparing with the negated entries for
C=\Lambda Q
in (
11
).
∎

Proposition 4.19
.

The cluster algebra structure
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
on
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}=\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}
is compatible with its standard Poisson structure.

Proof.

Using Lemma
4.15
, we can determine the Poisson coefficient matrix for the cluster variables
A_{j}=\varphi_{j}
in the seed
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
. We then utilise the criterion in Lemma
2.11
.

Applying Lemma
4.17
, and (
12
), up to reordering indices, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{T}\Omega

\displaystyle=\left(\left[I_{\mathrm{uf}}\;\;0\right]\Lambda Q^{T}\Lambda^{T}\right)\left((\Lambda^{T})^{-1}\left(\varDelta Q^{-1}-(Q^{T})^{-1}\varDelta\right)\Lambda^{-1}\right),

\displaystyle=\left[I_{\mathrm{uf}}\;\;0\right]\left(\Lambda Q^{T}\varDelta(Q^{-1}\Lambda^{-1})-\Lambda\varDelta\Lambda^{-1}\right),

\displaystyle=\left[I_{\mathrm{uf}}\;\;0\right]\left(\Lambda Q^{T}\varDelta(Q^{-1}\Lambda^{-1})-\varDelta\right),

\displaystyle=\left[I_{\mathrm{uf}}\;\;0\right]\varDelta\cdot\left(\left(\varDelta^{-1}\Lambda Q^{T}\varDelta\right)\left(Q^{-1}\Lambda^{-1}\right)-I_{\ell}\right).

Then applying Lemma
4.18
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{T}\Omega

\displaystyle=\left[I_{\mathrm{uf}}\;\;0\right]\varDelta\left(-\Lambda QQ^{-1}\Lambda^{-1}-I_{\ell}\right),

\displaystyle=-2\left[I_{\mathrm{uf}}\;\;0\right]\varDelta

which is comprised of a diagonal block and a zero block. Thus, the exchange matrix
\widetilde{E}
and the Poisson coefficient matrix
\Omega
form a compatible pair in the sense of
[
3
]
and Lemma
2.11
says the cluster and Poisson structures are compatible.
∎

Corollary 4.20
.

The cluster structure constructed on braid varieties in
[
6
]
and
[
18
]
is compatible with the standard Poisson structure.

Proof.

Since the left inductive weave of the braid variety
X(\underline{w_{0}},\underline{w})
the seed for the Bott–Samelson cell, we have the statement in the case that
w_{0}
is a prefix of the braid word by Corollary
4.19
.

As shown in the proofs of
[
6
, Theorem 5.22]
and its component lemmas, reachable seeds of
X(\underline{w})
are obtained by iterated freezings of seeds from
X(\underline{w_{0}},\underline{w})
, together with deletions by specialising frozen variables. These freezings and deletions commute with the mutation sequence to reach said seed, and therefore log-canonicity of the clusters inherited.
∎

5.
Weaves for non-full Bott–Samelson charts

5.1.
New vertices with frayed strands

In Section
4.5
, checking the Poisson-ness of trivalent vertices was made substantially easier by first factoring the rational map through an adjacent (and not-fully-supported) Bott–Samelson chart. This motivates a construction of weaves for an arbitrary Bott–Samelson chart. We show that non-full Bott–Samelson charts have canonical Poisson maps between them which allows us to find a cluster structure on each Bott–Samelson chart.

Recall that in a classical Demazure weave, we used
I
-coloured strands to separate flags which are in relative position
s_{i}
. For the Bott–Samelson charts, our negative letters indicate when two consecutive flags might be equal, or in relative position
s_{i}
, but not opposite. For this, we will introduce new strands coloured by labels in
-I
. These are informally referred to as
frayed strands
. In diagrams, these are denoted with dashed lines, which are meant to indicate how flags on regions separated by a dashed line can be equal when its corresponding affine parameter is zero.

Figure
2
shows an expanded set of permitted vertices for simply-laced type. These will be further expanded upon in Appendix
A
, but the arguments in this section will only require these initial six.

Figure 2
.
An expanded set of permitted vertices involving frayed strands.

Figure 3
.
Factoring a classical trivalent vertex as a fraying followed by a twining.

In Figure
2
, we will call the top-right vertex a
fraying
. The bottom-right vertex will be called a
twining
. Similar to the trivalent vertices in Figure
1
, fraying vertices have a rightward raking ray which reminds us to modify the variables of strands to the right. The trivalent vertices of the previous section will be replaced with a fraying followed immediately with a twining (see Figure
3
), but we will also be able to twine frayed strands which did not originate from a fraying. There are two new vertices involving frayed strands in the bottom row of Figure
2
, and their associated maps between charts are based on the following six matrix identities:

(13)

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})B_{j}(z_{2})B_{i}(z_{3})

\displaystyle=B_{j}(z_{3})B_{i}(z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2})B_{j}(z_{1}),

(14)

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})B_{k}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=B_{k}(z_{2})B_{i}(z_{1}),

(15)

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})

\displaystyle=u_{-i}(z_{1}^{-1})\alpha^{\vee}_{i}(z_{1})u_{i}(-z_{1}^{-1}),

(16)

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})B_{j}(z_{2})u_{-i}(z_{3})

\displaystyle=u_{-}j(z_{3})B_{i}(z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3})B_{j}(z_{2}),

(17)

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})u_{-k}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=u_{-k}(z_{2})B_{i}(z_{1})\,\;\text{ and}

(18)

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})u_{-i}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=B_{i}(z_{1}-z_{2}).

Remark 5.1
.

In
[
7
, §2.3, see also Theorem 5.18]
, there is a process of opening crossings to obtain maps between Bott–Samelson cells. The difference with the fraying vertex corresponding to (
15
) is that we do not (yet) propagate the lower triangular matrix leftwards. Our resulting map
\mathcal{O}^{(\dots,i,\dots)}\dasharrow\mathcal{O}^{(\dots,-i,\dots)}
is one between Bott–Samelson charts where the index at the relevant position has flipped sign and is the rational change of coordinates between adjacent Bott–Samelson charts.

Proposition 5.2
.

Suppose
i
and
j
are adjacent and
i
and
k
are not. We have Poisson maps between
2
and
3
dimensional Bott–Samelson charts given by

\mathcal{O}^{(i,-k)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-k,i)},\quad\Phi_{(i,-k)}(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto\Phi_{(-k,i)}(z_{2},z_{1})

and

\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-j,i,j)},\quad\Phi_{(i,j,-i)}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto\Phi_{(-j,i,j)}(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3},z_{2}).

Proof.

We use the Elek–Lu formula to check that these maps are Poisson with respect to the standard Poisson structures on these cells. Similarly to the classical case, the length
2
relation is Poisson as the bracket is trivial on both the domain and codomain.

Let
\gamma=(i,j,-i)
and
\widetilde{\gamma}=(-j,i,j)
, and consider the map
f:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}
defined by
\Phi_{\gamma}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto\Phi_{\widetilde{\gamma}}(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3},z_{2})
.
We can use the Elek–Lu formula to calculate the bracket on the domain
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
:

\{z_{1},z_{2}\}_{\gamma}=-z_{1}z_{2},\quad\{z_{1},z_{3}\}_{\gamma}=-z_{1}z_{3}+2z_{2}z_{3}^{2},\quad\{z_{2},z_{3}\}_{\gamma}=z_{2}z_{3},

and on the codomain
\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}
:

\{\widetilde{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{2}\}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}=\widetilde{z}_{1}\widetilde{z}_{2},\quad\{\widetilde{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{3}\}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}=-\widetilde{z}_{1}\widetilde{z}_{3},\quad\{\widetilde{z}_{2},\widetilde{z}_{3}\}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}=-\widetilde{z}_{2}\widetilde{z}_{3}.

Now explicitly checking,

\displaystyle f^{*}\left(\{\widetilde{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{2}\}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}\right)

\displaystyle=f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{1}\widetilde{z}_{2})=z_{3}(z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3})=z_{1}z_{3}-2z_{2}z_{3}^{2}+z_{2}z_{3}^{3},

\displaystyle=\{z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3}\}_{\gamma}=\{f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{1}),f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{2})\}_{\gamma},

\displaystyle f^{*}\left(\{\widetilde{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{3}\}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}\right)

\displaystyle=f^{*}(-\widetilde{z}_{1}\widetilde{z}_{3})=-z_{3}z_{2},

\displaystyle=\{z_{3},z_{2}\}_{\gamma}=\{f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{1}),f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{3})\}_{\gamma},\qquad\text{ and}

\displaystyle f^{*}\left(\{\widetilde{z}_{2},\widetilde{z}_{3}\}_{\widetilde{\gamma}}\right)

\displaystyle=f^{*}(-\widetilde{z}_{2}\widetilde{z}_{3})=-(z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3})z_{2}=-z_{1}z_{2}+z_{2}^{2}z_{3},

\displaystyle=\{z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3},z_{2}\}_{\gamma}=\{f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{2}),f^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{3})\}_{\gamma}.

and so the map
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}
is a Poisson map.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

The map
\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-j,i,j)}
is an isomorphism. We will not need its inverse for now, but it will give another permitted vertex in Section
A
.

Both these above maps respect the multiplication maps on these cells. Therefore the argument in Proposition
4.9
can be repeated, and we can apply these moves within a signed expression to “bring the negative letter leftward”.
The following lemma gives some equivalences for these moves. They are diagrammatically shown in Figures
4
and
5
.

Lemma 5.4
.

When
i,j
are adjacent roots, the compositions

\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i,-j)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(i,-i,j,i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i)}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i,-j)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(j,i,j,-j)}\to\mathcal{O}^{j,i,j}\to\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i)}

give the same Poisson map between the Bott–Samelson charts
\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i,-j)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i)}
.

For non-adjacent roots
i
and
k
, the compositions

\mathcal{O}^{(i,k,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(k,i,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(k,i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(i,k)}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{O}^{(i,k,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(i,-i,k)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(i,k)}

give the same map
\mathcal{O}^{(i,k,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(i,k)}
.

Proof.

This is checked by composing the relevant maps, see Figures
4
and
5
.
∎

Figure 4
.
Equivalent frayed weaves from
(i,k,-i)
to
(i,k)
.

Figure 5
.
Equivalent frayed weaves from
(i,j,i,-j)
to
(i,j,i)
.

Corollary 5.5
.

In simply-laced type, we can apply leftward moves as described above to get from the signed expression
(\underline{w_{0}},-i)
to
(i^{*},-i^{*},\underline{v})
, where
v=w_{0}s_{i}
and
i^{*}
is such that
s_{i^{*}}=w_{0}s_{i}w_{0}
.

Proof.

From any reduced word for
w_{0}
, we use a sequence of braid moves and commutations to get to a word
\underline{w_{0}}
which ends with
i
. Apply the trivalent twining vertex, and then apply the reversed sequence of braid moves and commutators to “undo” the coordinate change. By our equivalences in
5.4
, we can equally write our weave as an appropriate sequence of leftward moves.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

In type
A
, a direct proof is possible by using a good choice of reduced word
\underline{w_{0}}
and appealing to a braid group argument. See example
[
19
, Lemma 2]
.

Remark 5.7
.

Although this relation appears like the braid identity
\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}^{-1}=\sigma_{j}^{-1}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}
, not all frayed weave vertices used will look like this. See Figure
8
in the next section.

Proposition 5.8
.

For a signed expression of the form
(w_{0},\gamma)
, the leftmost frayed strand corresponding to the leftmost negative letter in
\gamma
can be twined by bringing it leftwards using an appropriate sequence of frayed weave moves.

Proof.

We use the procedure in
[
7
, Definition 5.15]
, where one chooses a word for
w_{0}
ending with
i
and then twining them.

Suppose
\gamma=(\underline{u},-i,\gamma^{\prime\prime})
, where
\underline{u}
is a sequence of positive letters. Pick a reduced word for
\underline{w_{0}}
for
w_{0}
which has
(\underline{u})^{*}
as a prefix. Then there is a sequence of braid moves between positive braids
(\underline{u},\underline{w_{0}})
and
((\underline{u})^{*},\underline{w_{0}})
. By Corollary
5.5
, we can bring the negative letter leftwards and twine it with the first letter in
\underline{w_{0}}
. Undoing the previous sequence of braid moves brings us back to
(\underline{w_{0}},\underline{u},\gamma^{\prime\prime})
.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

This procedure is similar to the opening crossing procedure which appears in
[
7
, §2.3, see also Theorem 5.18]
. The difference here is that we might already begin with a lower triangular factor to bring leftward which did not originate from fraying a strand.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
\gamma
be a subexpression of
\underline{w}
and let
\gamma^{+}=(\gamma_{i})_{\gamma_{i}>0}
. Then the Bott–Samelson chart
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
, where
m
is the number of unsupported letters in
\gamma
.
Endowing the product with a Poisson bracket which is log-canonical with respect to the Bott–Samelson coordinates, this becomes a Poisson isomorphism.

Proof.

From the isomorphism in Lemma
3.6
, we can replace the open part of the Bott–Samelson cell with the braid variety by left concatenation by the longest element
w_{0}
. Using our braid moves, we can iteratively bring the left-most frayed strand as far left as possible, before twining them with a strand of the same colour as in Lemma
4.12
and Proposition
5.8
to obtain a log-canonical product.
This gives us an isomorphism on the open subsets

\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\cap m^{-1}(B^{-}B/B)\cong(\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}\cap m^{-1}(B^{-}B/B))\times\mathbb{A}^{m},

and the isomorphism can be extended to the whole spaces
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\cong\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
since each of the component maps is regular in the Bott–Samelson coordinates.
∎

Let
\rho:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
denote the map given by composing the above isomorphism with the projection to the first factor.
We define the seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma)
to be the pullback of the seed on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
by
\rho
together with isolated frozens corresponding to the unsupported letters. Explicitly, if
j_{1}<\dots<j_{m}
are the unsupported positions in
\gamma
, the seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma)
is given by:

•

Cluster variables
(\rho^{*}(A_{j}))_{A_{j}\in\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+})}
together with the coordinates
(z_{j_{k}})_{k=1}^{m}
.

•

Frozen variables are the frozens from
\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+})
together with the coordinates
(z_{j_{k}})_{k=1}^{m}
.

•

Exchange matrix given by that from
\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+})
extended by zeroes.

Theorem 5.11
.

For
G
is simply-laced, the seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma)
endows the Bott–Samelson chart
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
with a cluster algebra structure,
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}(\gamma),\emptyset)=\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}]
. Furthermore, this is compatible with the standard Poisson structure.

Proof.

By the isomorphism in
5.10
, we have

\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}]\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}][z_{i_{k}}]_{k=1}^{m}
.
The cluster algebra structure on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
is that from
[
34
]
or
[
6
]
, and is known that
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+}),\emptyset)=\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}]
. Hence,

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}(\gamma),\emptyset)\cong\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+}),\emptyset)[z_{i_{k}}]_{k=1}^{m}=\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}][z_{i_{k}}]_{k=1}^{m}

under the same isomorphism from Proposition
5.10
.
Composing with the inverse, we have that
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{s}(\gamma),\emptyset)=\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}]
as subalgebras of the field of rational functions.

Compatibility with the standard Poisson structure follows because the cluster structure on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
is compatible with the standard Poisson structure by Corollary
4.20
and the map in Proposition
5.10
is a Poisson isomorphism with a log-canonical product.
∎

Corollary 5.12
.

The projection map
\rho:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
is a quasi-cluster morphism.

Proof.

The algebra map is given by embedding the seeds of
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
into copies of the same seed with extra isolated frozen vertices. The statement follows as the extra vertices do not affect the exchange ratios of mutable vertices.
∎

Lemma 5.13
.

The embedding
\sigma:\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
is a section to the projection map
\rho
.

Proof.

Denote the Bott–Samelson coordinates on
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}
by
z_{i}
and
\widetilde{z_{i}}
respectively. By construction, the
\rho^{*}(\widetilde{z_{j}})\in\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell}]
are of the form
\rho^{*}(\widetilde{z_{j}})=z_{j}-p(z_{j+1},\dots,z_{\ell-1})\cdot z_{\ell}
, where
p\in\mathbb{C}[z_{j+1},\dots,z_{\ell-1}]
is some polynomial.
The embedding
\sigma
is defined by
\Phi_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell-1})\mapsto\Phi_{\underline{w}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell-1},0)
, which becomes the identity map after composing by
\rho
.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

We emphasise that this morphism
\rho:\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}\to\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}
is
not
the truncation morphism
\psi_{\underline{w}}
despite
\sigma
being a section to both
\rho
and
\psi_{\underline{w}}
. See Example
6.15
for the construction in an explicit case.

Example 5.15
.

In type
A
, let
\gamma=(2,1,3,-2,2,-1)
so that
\gamma^{+}=(2,1,3,2)
. For Bott–Samelson coordinates
z_{1},\dots,z_{6}
on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
, applying the above procedure to the first negative letter,
-2
in the fourth position, yields the map

\mathcal{O}^{(2,1,3,-2,2,-1)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(2,3,1,2,-1)},\quad(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4},z_{5},z_{6})\mapsto(z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3}z_{4},z_{2},z_{3},z_{5},z_{6}).

Applying the procedure to the second negative letter,
-1
in the fifth (formerly sixth) position, yields the map

\mathcal{O}^{(2,1,3,2,-1)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(2,3,1,2)},\quad(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime},z_{3}^{\prime},z_{4}^{\prime},z_{5}^{\prime})\mapsto(z_{1}^{\prime}-z_{2}^{\prime}z_{5}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime},z_{3}^{\prime}-z_{4}^{\prime}z_{5}^{\prime},z_{5}^{\prime}).

These compose to get the map

\rho:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}},\quad(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4},z_{5},z_{6})\mapsto(z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3}z_{4}-z_{2}z_{6},z_{2},z_{3}-z_{5}z_{6},z_{5}),

and a weave representing this is shown in Figure
6
.

If we continued the weave in Figure
6
kept reducing down to the Demazure product of
w_{0}
, this would give us a log-canonical toric chart in
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\cap m^{-1}(B^{-}B/B)
, similar to Remark
3.1
. The Lusztig cycle procedure from
[
6
]
would give us a quiver for the corresponding toric chart in
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\cap m^{-1}(B^{-}B/B)\subset^{\gamma}
. In this sense, pulling back the cluster structure by
\rho
comes from precomposing a weave
(\underline{w_{0}},\gamma^{+})\to\underline{w_{0}}
by our “frayed weave”
(\underline{w}_{0},\gamma)\to(\underline{w_{0}},\gamma^{+})
.
If we chose the right inductive weave, the seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+})
on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma+}=\mathcal{O}^{(2,3,1,2)}
would be:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{1}z_{4}-z_{2}z_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{3}}}}

where the boxed functions denote frozen variables, and the dashed arrows are half-weighted.
For comparison, the corresponding seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma)=\rho^{*}(\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+}))\sqcup\left\{\boxed{z_{4}},\boxed{z_{6}}\right\}
on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
is:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{4}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3}z_{4}-z_{5}z_{6}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{(z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3}z_{4}-z_{5}z_{6})z_{5}-z_{2}(z_{3}-z_{5}z_{6})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{6}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{3}-z_{5}z_{6}}}}

Figure 6
.
A frayed weave
(\underline{w_{0}},2,3,1,-2,2,-1)\to(\underline{w_{0}},2,3,1,2)
representing the map
\rho:\mathcal{O}^{(2,3,1,-2,2,-1)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(2,3,1,2)}
. For convenience, we’ve chosen the reduced word
\underline{w_{0}}=(2,1,3,2,1,3)
for the longest element to minimise braid moves. The map does not depend on the choice of word for
w_{0}
nor on the coordinates for those strands.

Remark 5.16
.

There has been some study of Legendrian links and braid varieties which have negative crossings (see
[
8
, §3.4]
). It is unclear to the author if there is any relation to our frayed strands, or whether there is another topological interpretation of frayed strands.

5.2.
Non-simply-laced types

Similar to the Demazure weaves in
[
6
]
, we can extend the notion of frayed weaves to non-simply-laced types by including new vertices of higher valency. These will correspond to maps between Bott–Samelson charts supported on a sub-root-system of types
B_{2}
or
G_{2}
.

Just as in
[
6
]
, we can understand higher valency vertices in weaves as a result of folding a simply-laced weave. This allows us to intuit what the extra vertices are required when we involve frayed strands. In type
B_{2}
we use folding from
A_{3}
to get octavalent vertices corresponding to braid matrix identities of length
4
. We state the relations and maps for the
B_{2}
case below and draw the corresponding weaves. The statements for
12
-valent vertices in type
G_{2}
folded from
D_{4}
are analogous.

Lemma 5.17
.

On a rank
2
root subsystem with Cartan submatrix
\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\
-2&2\end{pmatrix}
, when
\underline{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})
, we have the following relations:

(19)

\displaystyle B_{(1,2,1,2)}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=B_{2}(z_{4})B_{1}(z_{1}z_{4}-z_{3})B_{2}(z_{1}^{2}z_{4}-2z_{1}z_{3}+z_{2})B_{1}(z_{1}),

(20)

\displaystyle B_{(2,1,2,1)}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=B_{1}(z_{4})B_{2}(z_{1}z_{4}^{2}-2z_{2}+z_{3}))B_{1}(z_{1}z_{4}-z_{2})B_{2}(z_{1}),

(21)

\displaystyle B_{(1,2,1,-2)}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=u_{-2}(z_{4})B_{1}(z_{1}-z_{3}z_{4})B_{2}(z_{2}-z_{3}^{2}z_{4})B_{1}(z_{3}),

(22)

\displaystyle B_{(2,1,2,-1)}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=u_{-1}(z_{4})B_{2}(z_{1}-2z_{2}z_{4}+z_{3}z_{4}^{2})B_{1}(z_{2}-z_{3}z_{4})B_{2}(z_{3}).

The corresponding octavalent vertices and their unfolded weaves are depicted in Figure
7
.

Proof.

These relations can seen by applying the previous simply-laced identities to an unfolded weave for type
B_{2}
, see Figure
7
. The formulas have been checked by an
SL_{4}
computation in SAGE.
∎

Note that how strand labels propagate in the non-simply-laced case depends on the relative lengths of the roots.

Figure 7
.
Frayed weaves in type
A_{3}
and the octavalent vertices they fold to in type
B_{2}
.

Lemma 5.18
.

When
i,j
form a
B_{2}
root subsystem, the compositions

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i,j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(i,-i,j,i,j)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i,j)}\;\text{ and }\;\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i,j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(j,i,j,i,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(j,i,j,i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i,j)}

give the same Poisson map between the Bott–Samelson charts
\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i,j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i,j)}
.

Proof.

This is analogous the first statement in Lemma
5.4
, and can be proved by lifting the relations to an appropriate unfolding weave and iteratively applying Lemma
5.4
.
∎

These statements allows us to construct the projection map
\rho:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
for any semisimple algebraic group.

Corollary 5.19
.

Theorem
5.11
holds when
G
is any semisimple algebraic group.

Example 5.20
.

In type
B_{2}
, with Cartan matrix
\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\
-2&2\end{pmatrix}
,
consider the signed expression
\gamma=(1,2,1,-2,2)
, so that
\gamma^{+}=(1,2,1,2)
. In the Bott–Samelson coordinates
z_{1},\dots,z_{4}
for
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
, the exchange matrix
E(\gamma^{+})
and seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+})
are:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Denoting the coordinates for
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
by
z_{i}^{\prime}
, the identities in Lemma
5.17
give the map

\rho^{*}:\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}]\to\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}],\quad(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})\mapsto(z_{1}^{\prime}-z_{3}^{\prime}z_{4}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime}-(z_{3}^{\prime})^{2}z_{4}^{\prime},z_{3}^{\prime},z_{5}^{\prime}).

Then the seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma)
is then:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{4}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{2}^{\prime}-(z_{3}^{\prime})^{2}z_{4}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{(z_{2}^{\prime}-(z_{3}^{\prime})^{2}z_{4}^{\prime})z_{5}^{\prime}-(z^{\prime}_{3})^{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z^{\prime}_{1}-z^{\prime}_{3}z^{\prime}_{4}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z^{\prime}_{1}z^{\prime}_{3}-z^{\prime}_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{2:1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1:2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1:2}}

Mutating at the first position yields the cluster variable
z_{3}^{\prime}=\rho^{*}(z_{3})
, and following this with mutation at the second position yields the cluster variable
z_{5}^{\prime}=\rho^{*}(z_{4})
.
Together with the initial cluster variables
z_{4}^{\prime}
,
z_{1}^{\prime}-z_{3}^{\prime}z_{4}^{\prime}
, and
z_{2}^{\prime}-(z_{3}^{\prime})^{2}z_{4}^{\prime}
these generate the polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}]
.

Remark 5.21
.

In unpublished work, it was previously observed by Jiang-Hua Lu and Milen Yakimov that the Bott–Samelson coordinates for any
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
are, up to a polynomial transformation, isomorphic to a symmetric Poisson CGL extension. This geometric interpretation using weaves to construct the isomorphism
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\cong\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
by factoring the maps through several other Bott–Samelson charts is new, and recovers their change of coordinates.

6.
Compatibility with transition functions

6.1.
Mutation sequences and pulling back seeds

Definition 6.1
.

For each position
1\leq j\leq\ell
, we define a sequence of mutations. Let
i=i_{j}
be the
a_{j}
-th appearance of
i
in
\underline{w}
and let
n_{i}
be the total number of appearances of
i
in
\underline{w}
. Then define
\widetilde{\mu}_{j}=\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\underline{w})
to be the mutation sequence

(23)

\widetilde{\mu}_{j}=\begin{cases}\mu_{(i,n_{i}-a_{j})}\circ\cdots\circ\mu_{(i,1)}&\text{ if }a_{j}<n_{i};\\
\mathrm{id}&\text{ if }a_{j}=n_{i},\end{cases}

and denote their composition by

(24)

\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}:=\widetilde{\mu}_{\ell}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\mu}_{2}\circ\widetilde{\mu}_{1}.

One thinks of this sequence as mutating in ascending order along the level of each letter, where we skip some final mutations dependent on the number of times we have already mutated at this level.

By
[
20
, §13.1]
or repeated applications of
[
34
, Proposition 3.25(1)]
(see also the formulation in
[
1
, Proposition 4.15]
), the sequence
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}

takes the seed
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
to the reversed seed
\mathbf{s}(\overline{i_{\ell}},\overline{i_{\ell-1}},\dots,\overline{i_{2}},\overline{i_{1}})
.
In fact
[
34
, Theorem 4.1]
proves that this is a maximal green sequence.
This is the seed obtained from weaves using the opposite opening sequence
(\ell,\ell-1,\dots,2,1)
, or the reversed proper reordering from CGL theory. Its cluster variables are given by the generalised minors of the products right aligned intervals:

(25)

\widetilde{A_{j}}=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{\ell-j+1}},\omega_{i_{\ell-j+1}}}(B_{\underline{w}[\ell-j+1,\ell]}(z_{\ell-j+1},\dots,z_{\ell})).

In particular, the first cluster variable
\widetilde{A_{1}}=z_{\ell}
is the last Bott–Samelson coordinate.

We now give an alternative mutation sequence which also reaches the reversed seed and will be useful later when comparing mutation sequences.

Definition 6.2
.

With the same notation as in Definition
6.1
, we also define the mutation sequences

(26)

\widehat{\mu}_{j}=\begin{cases}\mu_{(i,1)}\circ\cdots\circ\mu_{(i,a_{j}-1)}&\text{ if }1<a_{j};\\
\mathrm{id}&\text{ if }a_{j}=1.\end{cases}

We define
\overrightarrow{\mu^{\prime}_{\underline{w}}}=\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\circ\cdots\circ\widehat{\mu}_{2}\circ\widehat{\mu}_{1}
.

Notice that by construction we have

(27)

\overrightarrow{\mu^{\prime}_{\underline{w}}}=\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\circ\cdots\circ\widehat{\mu}_{2}\circ\widehat{\mu}_{1}=\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\circ\cdots\circ\widehat{\mu}_{j+1}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu^{\prime}_{\underline{w[j]}}}

Lemma 6.3
.

The two seeds

\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))=\widetilde{\mu}_{\ell}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\mu}_{1}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))\quad\text{and}\quad\overrightarrow{\mu^{\prime}_{\underline{w}}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))=\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\circ\cdots\circ\widehat{\mu}_{1}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))

are both equal to
\mathbf{s}(\overline{i_{\ell}},\overline{i_{\ell-1}},\dots,\overline{i_{2}},\overline{i_{1}})
.

Proof.

Inductively assuming the statement for the truncated word
\underline{w}[\ell-1]
, we have

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mu^{\prime}_{\underline{w}}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{s}(\overline{i_{\ell-1}},\dots,\overline{i_{2}},\overline{i_{1}},i_{\ell})\right),

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mu}_{\ell}\circ(\mu_{(i_{\ell},n_{i_{l}})}\circ\dots\circ\mu_{(i_{\ell},1)})\left(\mathbf{s}(\overline{i_{\ell}},\overline{i_{\ell-1}},\dots,\overline{i_{2}},\overline{i_{1}})\right),

\displaystyle=\mathbf{s}(\overline{i_{\ell}},\overline{i_{\ell-1}},\dots,\overline{i_{2}},\overline{i_{1}}).\qed

Remark 6.4
.

It is also possible to prove the coincidence of the two seeds by commuting mutations of non-adjacent vertices. Alternatively, one checks that both sequences are layered
T
-systems in the sense of Yakimov
[
36
]
, and hence both are maximal green sequences.

Remark 6.5
.

The sequence
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}
has a nice interpretation in terms of triangulations in configurations of flags, see
[
34
, Proposition 4.2]
.
The sequence
\overrightarrow{\mu^{\prime}_{\underline{w}}}
can be interpreted as doing the same but in the opposite direction, iteratively swapping letters
i
to their opposites
\overline{i}
and bringing them leftward instead of rightward.

Example 6.6
.

We illustrate this sequence with an example. In type
A_{2}
, let
\underline{w}=(1,2,1,2,1,2)
and
\gamma=(1,2,1,2,1,-2)
. The seed
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
looks like

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 4}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{5}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{5}}}

Calculating generalised minors for the partial products, the cluster is given by

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=z_{1},\quad A_{2}=z_{2},\quad A_{3}=z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2},\quad A_{4}=z_{2}z_{4}-z_{3},

\displaystyle A_{5}

\displaystyle=z_{1}z_{3}z_{5}-z_{2}z_{5}-z_{1}z_{4}+1,\quad A_{6}=z_{2}z_{4}z_{6}-z_{3}z_{6}-z_{2}z_{5}+1.

Our two forms of the reversing sequence are
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}=\mathrm{id}\circ\mathrm{id}\circ(\mu_{2})\circ(\mu_{1})\circ(\mu_{4}\circ\mu_{2})\circ(\mu_{3}\circ\mu_{1})
and
\overrightarrow{\mu^{\prime}_{\underline{w}}}=\mathrm{id}\circ\mathrm{id}\circ(\mu_{2}\circ\mu_{4})\circ(\mu_{1}\circ\mu_{3})\circ(\mu_{2})\circ(\mu_{1})
Applying either of these sequences to
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
yields the reversed seed:

\hbox to171.67pt{\vbox to32.03pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 85.8334pt\lower-16.01387pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

with mutable variables
A_{1}=z_{5}
,
A_{2}=z_{6}
,
A_{3}=z_{3}z_{5}-z_{4}
, and
A_{4}=z_{4}z_{6}-z_{5}
.

For any
\underline{w}
, we also define the sequence
\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}
to be the reversed sequence of mutations, so that
\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))=\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\gamma=\underline{w}(\ell)=(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell-1},-i_{\ell})
, be the subexpression obtained by negating the last letter.
Let
(A_{j}^{+})_{j=1}^{\ell-1}
be the cluster variables of the seed
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}[\ell-1]))
on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
and
(\widetilde{A_{j}})_{j=1}^{\ell}
be the cluster variables of the seed
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
on
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
.
Then as rational functions on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
, under the pullback by the map
\rho:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
, we have

\rho^{*}(A_{j}^{+})=\begin{cases}\widetilde{A_{j+1}}&\text{if }i_{\ell-j+1}\neq i_{\ell},\\
{\widetilde{A_{j+1}}}\cdot{z_{\ell}^{-1}}&\text{if }i_{\ell-j+1}=i_{\ell}.\end{cases}

Proof.

We use the expression of the cluster variables in
25
and applying the identity corresponding to fraying a strand. For
j>1
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{A_{j}}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{\ell-j+1}},\omega_{i_{\ell-j+1}}}(B_{\underline{w}[\ell-j+1,\ell]}(z_{\ell-j+1},\dots,z_{\ell})),

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{\ell-j+1}},\omega_{i_{\ell-j+1}}}(B_{\underline{w}[\ell-j+1,\ell-1]}(z_{\ell-j+1},\dots,z_{\ell-1})B_{i_{\ell}}(z_{\ell})),

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{\ell-j+1}},\omega_{i_{\ell-j+1}}}(B_{\underline{w}[\ell-j+1,\ell-1]}(z_{\ell-j+1},\dots,z_{\ell-1})u_{-i_{\ell}}(z_{\ell}^{-1})\alpha_{i_{\ell}}^{\vee}(z_{\ell})u_{i_{\ell}}(-z_{\ell}^{-1})),

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{\ell-j+1}},\omega_{i_{\ell-j+1}}}(B_{\underline{w}[\ell-j+1,\ell-1]}(z^{\prime}_{\ell-j+1},\dots,z^{\prime}_{\ell-1})\alpha_{i_{\ell}}^{\vee}(z_{\ell}))

where the
z^{\prime}_{i}
arise from the coordinate change from bringing
u_{-i_{\ell}}(z_{\ell}^{-1})
to the left. This is the definition of the map
\rho
, and therefore

\displaystyle\widetilde{A_{j}}

\displaystyle=\rho^{*}(A_{j-1}^{+})\cdot\Delta_{\omega_{i_{\ell-j+1}},\omega_{i_{\ell-j+1}}}(\alpha_{i_{\ell}}^{\vee}(z_{\ell}))

which yields the result.
∎

Proposition 6.8
.

The freezing of the seed
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
on
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
at the first cluster variable
\widetilde{A_{1}}=z_{\ell}
is quasi-equivalent to the seed
on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}=\mathcal{O}^{(i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell-1},-i_{\ell})}
given by

\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}}(\mathbf{s}(\gamma))=\rho^{*}(\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}[\ell-1])))\sqcup\{\boxed{z_{\ell}^{\prime}}\}.

Proof.

Our seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma)
for
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
has one extra frozen vertex whose variable is
z_{\ell}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{z_{\ell}}
. By Lemma
6.7
, we can rescale the variables
\rho^{*}(A_{j}^{+})
by a monomial in this frozen variable to get the desired cluster variables
\widetilde{A_{j}}
. It remains to check that doing so preserves the exchange ratios at each mutable vertex
k
.
From Lemma
6.7
, the degree of
z_{\ell}
in the exchange ratio at vertex
k
will be

D_{k}=-\sum_{j}\varepsilon_{jk}\delta_{i_{\ell-j-1},i_{\ell}},\quad\text{where}\quad\delta_{i_{\ell-j-1},i_{\ell}}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }i_{\ell-j-1}=i_{\ell},\\
0&\text{if }i_{\ell-j-1}\neq i_{\ell}.\end{cases}

First consider the case where
i_{k}\neq i_{\ell}
. From the structure of the exchange matrix for the seed
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}[\ell-1]))=\mathbf{s}(\overline{i_{\ell-1}},\dots,\overline{i_{1}})=\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
, exactly one of the following occurs for the column
(\varepsilon_{1,k},\dots,\varepsilon_{\ell,k})^{T}
:

(1)

The column is such that
\varepsilon_{j,k}=0
whenever
i_{j}=i_{\ell}
, and in this case
D_{k}=0
.

(2)

There are exactly two indices
j_{1}<k<j_{2}
with
i_{j_{1}}=i_{j_{2}}=i_{\ell}
with
\varepsilon_{j_{1},k},\varepsilon_{j_{2},k}\neq 0
. We have
j_{1}<k<s(j_{1})
,
k<j_{2}<s(k)
, so
\varepsilon_{j_{1},k}=a_{i_{k},i_{\ell}}=-\varepsilon_{j_{2},k}
, and thus
D_{k}=0
.

(3)

There is exactly one index
j
with
i_{j}=i_{\ell}
and
\varepsilon_{j,k}\neq 0
. In this case,
k<j<s(k)
,
\varepsilon_{j,k}=a_{i_{k},i_{\ell}}
and
D_{k}=-a_{i_{k},i_{\ell}}
.

A similar consideration when
i_{k}=i_{\ell}
shows that for mutable vertices
(i_{\ell},m)
with
m>1
on level
i_{\ell}
, they are all have
-\varepsilon_{p(k),k}=1=\varepsilon_{s(k),k}
, and therefore
D_{k}=0
. But there is one vertex, namely
(i_{\ell},1)
the first vertex on level
i_{\ell}
which has no predecessor, and for this index we have
D_{k}=-1
.

Therefore, the degree
D_{k}
is zero at any mutable vertex
k
except for those indices in case (iii) and the first index in the level
i_{\ell}
. In these cases, the
D_{k}
is precisely equal to the
-a_{i_{k},i_{\ell}}
and
-1
respectively. Therefore, to preserve exchange ratios when rescaling the factor of
z_{\ell}
in the cluster variables, we add
D_{k}
arrows from the frozen vertex
\boxed{z_{\ell}}
to vertex
k
.
This recovers the freezing of
z_{\ell}
in the seed
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
.
∎

Proposition 6.9
.

For
\gamma=\underline{w}(\ell)=(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell-1},-i_{\ell})
, there exists a rational quasi-cluster morphism
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}\to\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}
such that the following picture is commutative

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}},\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}[\ell-1]},\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}[\ell-1])).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigl(J(\underline{w}[\ell-1]){,}\{(i_{\ell},1)\}\bigr)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{1}}

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
6.7
, Corollary
6.8
, and applying the reversing sequence
\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}}
to both seeds.
∎

Note that there is a change of the indexing in the morphism. The embedding of seed vertices
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}[\ell-1]))\to\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
is given by
(i,m)\mapsto(i,m)
if
i\neq i_{\ell}
and
(i,m)\mapsto(i,m+1)
if
i=i_{\ell}
, so the second mutation sequence factor
\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}}
is applied to vertices in level
i_{\ell}
shifted by one position.

Example 6.10
.

Continuing Example
6.6
and applying
\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}}=(\mu_{1}\circ\mu_{3})\circ\mu_{4}\circ\mu_{1}\circ\mathrm{id}\circ\mathrm{id}
to the last seed, we get
