with mutable variables
A_{2}=z_{6}
,
A_{1}=z_{1}
,
A_{4}=z_{2}
, and
A_{3}=(z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2})z_{6}-z_{1}
. Note the copy of the quiver for
\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+})
supported on the vertices
J\setminus\{2\}=J\setminus\{(2,1)\}
.

If
\underline{v}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
is a prefix of
\underline{w}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell})
with
m<\ell
, there is an embedding of seeds
\mathbf{s}(\underline{v})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
. This means we can apply the mutation sequence
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{v}}}
to
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
to get the seed
\mathbf{s}(\overline{i_{m}},\overline{i_{m-1}},\dots,\overline{i_{2}},\overline{i_{1}},i_{m+1},\dots,i_{\ell})
, whose cluster variables are

(28)

\widetilde{A_{j}}=\begin{cases}\Delta_{\omega_{i_{m-j+1}},\omega_{i_{m-j+1}}}(B_{\underline{w}[m-j+1,m]}(z_{m-j+1},\dots,z_{m}))&\text{ if }1\leq j\leq m,\\
\Delta_{\omega_{i_{j}},\omega_{i_{j}}}(B_{\underline{w}[j]}(z_{1},\dots,z_{j}))&\text{ if }m<j\leq\ell.\end{cases}

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
\gamma=\underline{w}(m)=(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{m-1},-i_{m},i_{m+1},\dots,i_{\ell})
, be the subexpression obtained by negating the
m
-th letter.
Let
(A_{j}^{+})_{j=1}^{\ell-1}
be the cluster variables of the seed
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[m-1]}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}[m-1],\underline{w}[m+1,\ell]))
on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
and
(\widetilde{A_{j}})_{j=1}^{\ell}
be the cluster variables of the seed
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}[m]}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
on
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
.
Then as rational functions on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
, under the pullback by the map
\rho:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
, we have

\rho^{*}(A_{j}^{+})=\begin{cases}\widetilde{A_{j+1}}&\text{if }i_{m-j+1}\neq i_{m}\text{ and }1\leq j<m,\\
\widetilde{A_{j+1}}\cdot z_{m}^{-1}&\text{if }i_{m-j+1}=i_{m}\text{ and }1\leq j<m,\\
\widetilde{A_{j+1}}\cdot z_{m}^{-\langle\underline{w}^{j}(\omega_{i_{j}}),\underline{w}^{m}(\alpha_{i_{m}}^{\vee})\rangle}&\text{if }m\leq j\leq\ell-1,\end{cases}

Proof.

Similar to the proof of Lemma
6.7
, we use the expression of the cluster variables in
28
and apply identities corresponding to fraying a strand.
The first two cases are the same as in the proof of Lemma
6.7
. For
m+1\leq j\leq\ell
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{A_{j}}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{j}},\omega_{i_{j}}}(B_{\underline{w}[j]}(z_{\ell-j+1},\dots,z_{\ell})),

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{j}},\omega_{i_{j}}}(B_{\underline{w}[m-1]}(z_{1},\dots,z_{m-1})B_{i_{m}}(z_{m})B_{\underline{w}[m+1,j]}(z_{m+1},\dots,z_{j})),

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{j}},\omega_{i_{j}}}(B_{\underline{w}[m-1]}(z_{1},\dots,z_{m-1})u_{-i_{m}}(z_{m}^{-1})\alpha_{i_{m}}^{\vee}(z_{m})u_{i_{m}}(-z_{m}^{-1})B_{\underline{w}[m+1,j]}(z_{m+1},\dots,z_{j})),

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\omega_{i_{j}},\omega_{i_{j}}}(B_{\underline{w}[m-1]}(z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,z^{\prime}_{m-1})B_{\underline{w}[m+1,j]}(z_{m+1}^{\prime},\dots,z_{j}^{\prime})((s_{i_{j}}\cdots s_{i_{m+1}})\cdot\alpha_{i_{m}})^{\vee}(z_{m})\cdot U)

where the
z^{\prime}_{i}
arise from the coordinate change from bringing
u_{-i_{\ell}}(z_{\ell}^{-1})
to the left and the Borel element
\alpha_{i_{m}}^{\vee}(z_{m})u_{i_{m}}(-z_{m}^{-1})
to the right. The first factor here matches the construction of the map
\rho
, and therefore

\displaystyle A_{j}

\displaystyle=\rho^{*}({A_{j-1}^{+}})\cdot\Delta_{\omega_{i_{j}},\omega_{i_{j}}}(((s_{i_{j}}\cdots s_{i_{m+1}})\cdot\alpha_{i_{m}})^{\vee}(z_{m})),

\displaystyle=\rho^{*}({A_{j-1}^{+}})\cdot\omega_{i_{j}}(((s_{i_{j}}\cdots s_{i_{m+1}})\cdot\alpha_{i_{m}})^{\vee}(z_{m})),

\displaystyle=\rho^{*}({A_{j-1}^{+}})\cdot z_{m}^{\langle\omega_{i_{j}},(s_{i_{j}}\cdots s_{i_{m+1}})(\alpha_{i_{m}}^{\vee})\rangle}.

which yields the result.
∎

Proposition 6.12
.

For
1\leq m\leq\ell
and
\gamma=\underline{w}(m)
, the freezing of the seed
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[m]}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
at the first cluster variable
\widetilde{A_{1}}=z_{m}
is quasi-equivalent to the seed on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
given by

\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[m-1]}}(\mathbf{s}(\gamma))=\rho^{*}(\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[m-1]}}(\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+})))\sqcup\{\boxed{z_{m}^{\prime}}\}.

Proof.

The proof is an extension of that from Proposition
6.8
. As before, we wish to rescale the cluster variables by appropriate powers of the frozen variable
z_{m}=\frac{1}{z_{m}^{\prime}}
. We therefore need to check the degree
D_{k}
of
z_{m}
in the exchange ratio at vertex
k
in the pulled back seed
\rho^{*}(\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[m-1]}}(\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+}))
.
The case for indices with
k<m
is checked in the proof of Proposition
6.8
, we now determine the discrepancy for mutable indices
k\geq m
. For
j>m
, let
\beta_{j}
denote the integer
-\langle\underline{w}^{j}(\omega_{i_{j}}),\underline{w}^{m}(\alpha_{i_{m}}^{\vee})\rangle=-\langle\omega_{i_{j}},(s_{i_{j}}\cdots s_{i_{m+1}})(\alpha_{i_{m}}^{\vee})\rangle
, and for
j<m
, let
\beta_{j}=-\delta_{i_{m-j-1},i_{m}}
as in Proposition
6.8
.
By the action of simple reflections,

\beta_{s(k)}=-\beta_{k}-\left(\sum_{k<j<s(k)<s(j)}a_{i_{k},i_{j}}\beta_{j}\right)\;\text{ and }\;\beta_{k}=-\beta_{p(k)}-\left(\sum_{j<k<s(j)<s(k)}a_{i_{k},i_{j}}\beta_{j}\right).

Then for
k
with one of
k>s(m)
or
s(k)<s(m)
, we have

\displaystyle D_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\varepsilon_{jk}\beta_{j},

\displaystyle=\beta_{s(k)}+\left(\sum_{k<j<s(k)<s(j)}a_{i_{k},i_{j}}\beta_{j}\right)-\beta_{p(k)}-\left(\sum_{j<k<s(j)<s(k)}a_{i_{k},i_{j}}\beta_{j}\right),

\displaystyle=-\beta_{k}+\beta_{k}=0.

So as in the proof of Proposition
6.8
, a nonzero discrepancy occurs only at positions
k
with
i_{k}=i_{m}
when
m<k<s(m)<s(k)
or the
k=s(m)
. In the case
k=s(m)
, we have

\displaystyle D_{k}

\displaystyle=-\beta_{k}-\left(\sum_{j<k<s(j)<s(k)}a_{i_{k},i_{j}}\beta_{j}\right)=\langle\omega_{i_{m}},\alpha_{i_{m}}^{\vee}\rangle=-1,

and when
m<k<s(m)<s(k)
we have
i_{k}\neq i_{m}
, and

\displaystyle D_{k}

\displaystyle=-\beta_{k}-\beta_{p(k)}-\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j<k<s(j)<s(k)\\
j\neq m\end{subarray}}a_{i_{k},i_{j}}\beta_{j}\right)=-a_{i_{k},i_{m}},

Adding these many arrows to/from the new frozen vertex therefore preserves the exchange ratios when rescaling to remove all the factors of
z_{m}
from the cluster variables.
∎

Remark 6.13
.

The above arguments were inspired by a definition of
regular pullback of seeds
under rational maps by Gekhtman–Shapiro–Vainshtein, see
[
23
, §2.3]
and
[
24
]
. The procedure here is slightly different as we rescale the mutable variables by all powers of the pole of the rational map, not just those factors which are in the denominator.

Corollary 6.14
.

For
\gamma=\underline{w}(j)=(i_{1},\dots,i_{j-1},-i_{j},i_{j+1},\dots,i_{\ell})
, we similarly have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}},\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[j-1]}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[j]}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}},\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}[j-1],\underline{w}[j+1,\ell])).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigl(J(\underline{w}[j-1],\underline{w}[j+1,\ell]){,}\{(i_{j},1)\}\bigr)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho_{1}}}

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
6.11
, Corollary
6.12
, and applying the reversing sequence
\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[j-1]}}
to both seeds.
∎

As before, there is a change of the indexing in the morphism. The embedding of seed vertices
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[m-1]}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}[m-1],\underline{w}[m+1,\ell]))\to\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w[m]}}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
is given by
(i,m)\mapsto(i,m)
if
i\neq i_{\ell}
and
(i,m)\mapsto(i,m+1)
if
i=i_{\ell}
, so the second mutation sequence factor
\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}}
is applied to vertices in level
i_{m}
shifted by one position.

Example 6.15
.

Continuing with Example
3.12
, consider the word and subexpression
\underline{w}=(1,2,1,2)
and
\gamma=(1,2,1,-2)
in type
A
. Note that
\gamma^{+}=\underline{w}[\ell-1]=(1,2,1)
.
Let the Bott–Samelson coordinates on the Bott–Samelson charts
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
,
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
, and
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
be given by
z_{i}
,
z_{i}^{\prime}
, and
\widetilde{z}_{i}
respectively.

The seed
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
on
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
is given in coordinates by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{2}z_{4}-z_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}}}}

One can compare this against the initial seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+})
on
\mathcal{O}^{{\gamma}^{+}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{\widetilde{z}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{z}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{\widetilde{z}_{1}\widetilde{z}_{3}-\widetilde{z}_{2}}}}

to see that the truncation map
\psi:\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}\to\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}
,
\psi^{*}(\widetilde{z}_{i})=z_{i}
is rational quasi-cluster.
Applying the mutation sequence
\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[\ell-1]}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}=\mu_{1}\circ(\mu_{2}\circ\mu_{1})
to
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
, we get the seed

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{4}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{2}z_{4}-z_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}z_{4}-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}}}}

This corresponds to finding an appropriate seed for
\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1,2)}
, so that we may freeze and isolate the coordinate
z_{4}
as one of our cluster variables.
The Poisson map described in Proposition
5.10
(see Figure
5
for example weaves) is given by

\rho:\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1-2)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1)},\quad\Phi_{\gamma}(z^{\prime}_{1},z^{\prime}_{2},z^{\prime}_{3},z^{\prime}_{4})\mapsto\Phi_{\gamma^{+}}(z^{\prime}_{1}-z^{\prime}_{4},z^{\prime}_{2}-z^{\prime}_{3}z^{\prime}_{4},z^{\prime}_{3}).

Observe that the embedding
\sigma:\mathring{Z}_{(1,2,1)}\hookrightarrow\mathring{Z}_{(1,2,1,2)}
given by
[g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}]\mapsto[g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},e]
restricts to a section of this map. Indeed, in the coordinates
\widetilde{z}_{i}
, the can be expressed as

\sigma:\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1)}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1,-2)},\quad\Phi_{\gamma^{+}}(\widetilde{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{2},\widetilde{z}_{3})\mapsto\Phi_{\gamma}(\widetilde{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{2},\widetilde{z}_{3},0).

Pulling back our cluster structure on
\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1)}
via
\rho
and adding an isolated frozen
z_{4}^{\prime}
, the initial seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma)
described in Theorem
5.11
is:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z^{\prime}_{2}-z^{\prime}_{3}z^{\prime}_{4}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z^{\prime}_{4}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z^{\prime}_{1}-z^{\prime}_{4}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z^{\prime}_{1}z^{\prime}_{3}-z^{\prime}_{2}}}}

Mutating at the first cluster variable
z^{\prime}_{1}-z^{\prime}_{4}
(resp.
z_{1}
) yields the variable
z_{3}^{\prime}
(resp.
z_{3}
). This makes it evident that the quotient by
z_{4}^{\prime}
is a quasi-cluster morphism
\sigma:\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1)}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1,-2)}
.

Using the coordinate change
z_{4}^{\prime}=z_{4}^{-1}
, observe that the exchange ratios for the bottom left variable are preserved between the initial seed of
\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1,-2)}
and that of
\mu_{1}\circ\mu_{2}\circ\mu_{1}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
, and the variables themselves differ by a monomial of the isolated frozen,

z_{1}^{\prime}-z_{4}^{\prime}=z_{4}^{\prime}(z_{1}z_{4}-1).

To summarise, we have the projection map
\rho:\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1-2)}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1)}
which is rational quasi-cluster. Its natural section,
\sigma:\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1)}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1-2)}
, is a quasi-cluster morphism given by taking the quotient by an isolated frozen variable. We also have the change of coordinates
\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1,2)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1,-2)}
, which is rational quasi-cluster and composes with
\rho
to give a rational quasi-cluster map
\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1,2)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1)}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1,2)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi_{\underline{w}}(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime},z_{3}^{\prime},(z_{4}^{\prime})^{-1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi_{\gamma}(\widetilde{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{2},\widetilde{z}_{3},0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1,-2)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi_{\gamma}(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime},z_{3}^{\prime},z_{4}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi_{\gamma^{+}}(\widetilde{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{2},\widetilde{z}_{3});0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1)}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi_{\gamma^{+}}(z_{1}^{\prime}-z_{4}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime}-z_{3}^{\prime}z_{4}^{\prime},z_{3}^{\prime});z_{4}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi_{\gamma^{+}}(\widetilde{z}_{1},\widetilde{z}_{2},\widetilde{z}_{3})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi_{\gamma^{+}}(z_{1}^{\prime}-z_{4}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime}-z_{3}^{\prime}z_{4}^{\prime},z_{3}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

Let
\gamma
be any subexpression of
\underline{w}
and let
j_{1}<\dots<j_{m}
be the indices where
\gamma_{j_{l}}<0
. The change of coordinates from
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
to
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
is obtained by a composition of adjacent charts

\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\gamma_{(1)}}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\gamma_{(2)}}\dashrightarrow\cdots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\gamma_{(m)}}=\mathcal{O}^{\gamma},

where
\gamma_{(l)}
denotes the subexpression of
\underline{w}
with
\gamma_{j_{l^{\prime}}}<0
for
l^{\prime}\leq l
, and
\gamma_{k}>0
else. This motivates the following definition which arises from composing the maps in Corollary
6.14
.

Definition 6.16
.

Let
\gamma
be any subexpression of
\underline{w}
and let
j_{1}<\dots<j_{m}
be the indices where
\gamma_{j_{l}}<0
.
Then we iteratively define the mutation sequence
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}
by

\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma_{(l)}}}=(\overleftarrow{\mu_{\gamma_{(l-1)}^{+}[j_{l}-1]}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma_{(l-1)}^{+}[j_{l}]}})\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma_{(l-1)}}},

so that

\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}=(\overleftarrow{\mu_{\gamma_{(m-1)}^{+}[j_{m}-m]}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma_{(m-1)}^{+}[j_{m}-(m-1)]}})\circ\cdots\circ(\overleftarrow{\mu_{\gamma_{(1)}^{+}[j_{2}-2]}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma_{(1)}^{+}[j_{2}-1]}})\circ(\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[j_{1}-1]}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[j_{1}]}}).

Example 6.17
.

Continuing Example
5.15
, let
\underline{w}=(2,1,3,2,2,1)
, so that
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
for
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
is:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}z_{4}-z_{2}z_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{(z_{1}z_{4}-z_{2}z_{3})z_{5}-z_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{3}z_{6}-z_{4}z_{5}+1}}}

The mutation sequence
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
gives the seed

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))

\displaystyle=(\overleftarrow{\mu_{\gamma_{(1)}^{+}[5]}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma_{(1)}^{+}[5]}})\circ(\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[3]}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[4]}})(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mu_{4}\circ\mu_{3}\circ\mu_{4}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathrm{id}\circ\mu_{1}\bigr)(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

which looks like:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{z_{4}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(z_{1}z_{4}-z_{2}z_{3})z_{6}-z_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{(z_{1}z_{4}-z_{2}z_{3})z_{5}-z_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{z_{6}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{3}z_{6}-z_{4}z_{5}+1}}}

Freezing the variables
z_{4}
and
z_{6}
, will give a seed quasi-equivalent to the seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma)
obtained in Example
5.15
, where we only need to rescale by the (now frozen) poles
z_{4}
,
z_{6}
of the transition function
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
.

Theorem 6.18
.

The seed
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
is an iterated pullback and defrosting of the seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+})
under the Poisson rational maps

\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\gamma_{(1)}^{+}}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\gamma_{(2)}^{+}}\dashrightarrow\cdots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}.

Furthermore, we have rational quasi-cluster morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}},\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}},\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+})).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigl(J(\gamma^{+}){,}S_{\gamma}\bigr)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho_{1}}}

arising from the embeddings of seeds
\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{s}(\gamma)\rightarrow\overline{\mu_{\gamma}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))
.

Proof.

This first part from the iterative definition of the sequence
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}
and the application of Proposition
6.12
to each composition factor.
The second part is the iterative application of Corollary
6.14
.
∎

Remark 6.19
.

The construction of the maps
\rho:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
presented in this paper relied on the existence of a longest element
w_{0}
and therefore does not extend to the Kac–Moody setting of
[
1
]
. We expect a Poisson isomorphism
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
to still exist, just not with this construction. The quasi-equivalence arguments in this section just use data from the Cartan matrix, and we expect this to carry though to the Kac–Moody setting.

Remark 6.20
.

Note different orderings of the unsupported letters of
\gamma
give different compositions of maps between of adjacent charts. These give the same rational map after the composition, but the component maps may have different poles.
The pullback of
\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+})
to
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
, therefore depends on the order in which we negate the letters.
This is also reflected in how the mutation sequences for the adjacent charts need not necessarily commute.

Example 6.21
.

Consider
\underline{w}=(1,1,1)
and
\gamma=(1,-1,-1)
.
The rational map
\mathcal{O}^{(\underline{w})}\dasharrow\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{(1)}
is given by

\Phi_{\underline{w}}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto\Phi_{(1)}\left(\frac{z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}-z_{1}z_{3}}{z_{2}z_{3}-1}\right).

In particular, the rational map only has one irreducible component of the denominators, and the regular pullback procedure from
[
23
,
24
]
would only add one additional vertex. Choosing a path of adjacent charts adds to the indeterminacy locus, and allows us to use the previous results to construct seeds for
\underline{w}=(1,1,1)
from those of
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
and
\mathcal{O}^{(1)}
.
Note however, different orderings of adjacent charts may yield different seeds.
The seed
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
is:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}z_{2}-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}-z_{1}-z_{3}}}}

Let
\gamma^{\prime}=(1,-1,1)
, applying the sequence
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{\prime}}}:=\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[1]}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[2]}}=\mathrm{id}\circ\mu_{1}
to
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
yields

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}z_{2}-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}-z_{1}-z_{3}}}}

whereas for
\gamma^{\prime\prime}=(1,1,-1)
, the sequence
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}}:=\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}[2]}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}=\mu_{2}\circ\mu_{1}\circ(\mu_{2}\circ\mu_{1})
gives

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}-z_{1}-z_{3}}}}

Composing the sequences with
\overleftarrow{\mu_{(\gamma)^{+}[1]}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{(\gamma^{\prime})^{+}}}=\overleftarrow{\mu_{(\gamma)^{+}[1]}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{(\gamma^{\prime\prime})^{+}}}=\mathrm{id}\circ\mu_{2}
, gives the seeds

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{2}z_{3}-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}-z_{1}-z_{3}}}}

in the first case, and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{2}z_{3}-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}-z_{1}-z_{3}}}}

in the second. The next subsection illustrates how this left-to-right choice is already present in the literature, but it might be interesting to study seeds arising from other orderings.

6.2.
Comparison with Ménard’s mutation sequence

In
[
30
]
, Ménard constructed a specific of mutations, freezings, and deletions which takes
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
to a seed for open Richardson varieties. Bao and Ye
[
1
]
generalised this algorithm for Kac–Moody open Richardson varieties
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}^{v}
in the twisted product of flags. Here, we show the relationship between the sequence
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}
defined in the previous section and Ménard’s mutation sequence
M(\gamma)
.

Definition 6.22

(Adapted from
[
30
, Definition 6.1]
and
[
1
, Definition 5.1]
)
.

As before, say the letter
i
appears
n_{i}
times in
\underline{w}
and for
1\leq j\leq\ell
let
i_{j}=i
be the
a_{j}
-th appearance of
i=i_{j}
in
\underline{w}
. Further define
b_{j}=|\{k<j\mid\gamma_{k}=-i\}|
to be the number of instances of
i
before position
j
that are
not
supported the subexpression.

Define
\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{j}=\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{j}(\gamma)
to be the mutation sequence

(29)

\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{j}=\begin{cases}\mu_{(i,n_{i}-(a_{j}-b_{j}))}\circ\cdots\circ\mu_{(i,b_{j}+1)}&\text{ if }a_{l}<n_{i}\text{ and }\gamma_{j}=i;\\
\mathrm{id}&\text{ if }\gamma_{j}=-i;\\
\mathrm{id}&\text{ if }a_{j}=n_{i}.\end{cases}

Finally, we define
M(\gamma)=\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell}\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell-1}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{2}\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{1}
.

As described in
[
30
]
, this sequence mutates in ascending order on each level for each supported letter, but skips some initial mutations for each unsupported letter and a number of final mutations dependent on how many times we have already mutated along level.

Remark 6.23
.

The results for open Richardson varieties in
[
30
,
1
]
require
\gamma
to be the left-most subexpression of some
\gamma^{+}=v\leq\delta(\underline{w})
, however the mutation sequence itself can be constructed for any subexpression
\gamma\in\Upsilon_{\underline{w}}
.

Lemma 6.24
.

Let
\gamma=(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma_{\ell})
be a subexpression of
\underline{w}=(\underline{w}^{\prime},i)
, and
b
be the number of instances of
-i
in
\gamma^{\prime}
.
Then
pushing forward Ménard’s sequence
M(\gamma^{\prime})
under the inclusion of seeds
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
,
we have

(30)

M(\gamma)(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))=\left(\mu_{(i,b+1)}\circ\dots\circ\mu_{(i,n_{i}-1)}\right)\circ M(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})).

Proof.

Consider the decompositions
M(\gamma)=\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{2}\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{1}
and
M(\gamma^{\prime})=\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell-1}^{\prime}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{2}^{\prime}\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{1}^{\prime}
. Note that
\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell}=\mathrm{id}
and for
j<\ell
, we have

\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{j}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{j}^{\prime}&\text{if }i_{j}\neq i,\\
\mu_{(i,n_{i}-(a_{j}-b_{j}))}\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{j}^{\prime}&\text{if }\gamma_{j}=i\neq-i.\end{cases}

Let
k_{1}<\dots<k_{n_{i}-b-1}=\ell
denote the positions in
\gamma^{\prime}
such that
\gamma_{k_{l}}=i=i_{\ell}
. It suffices to argue that we can apply commutations to bring the
n_{i}-b-1
mutations from the first factor in (
30
) adjacent to its respective instance of
\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{k_{l}}^{\prime}
.

From the description of Ménard’s algorithm for open Richardson seeds in
[
1
, §5]
, after the application of
\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{k_{l}}^{\prime}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{2}^{\prime}\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{1}^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
, we never again mutate at any vertex adjacent to
(i,n_{i}-k_{l}+1)
in the rest of the sequence
M(\gamma^{\prime})
.
This means the star neighbourhood of
(i,n_{i}-k_{l}+1)
is preserved, and we can iteratively apply commutations to get the equalities

\displaystyle\bigl(\mu_{(i,b+1)}

\displaystyle\circ\dots\circ\mu_{(i,n_{i}-2)}\circ\mu_{(i,n_{i}-1)}\bigr)\circ M(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=\left(\mu_{(i,b+1)}\circ\dots\circ\mu_{(i,n_{i}-2)}\circ\mu_{(i,n_{i}-1)}\right)\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell-1}^{\prime}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{2}^{\prime}\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{1}^{\prime}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=\left(\mu_{(i,b+1)}\circ\dots\circ\mu_{(i,n_{i}-2)}\right)\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell-1}^{\prime}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{k_{1}+1}^{\prime}\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{k_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{1}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=\left(\mu_{(i,b+1)}\circ\dots\circ\mu_{(i,n_{i}-3)}\right)\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell-1}^{\prime}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{k_{2}+1}^{\prime}\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{k_{2}}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{1}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle\;\,\vdots

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell-1}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{k_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{1}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell}\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{\ell-1}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{k_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{\mu}}_{1}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=M(\gamma)(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})).\qed

Lemma
6.24
implies that
Ménard’s sequence
M(\gamma)
applied to
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
yields the same seed as the application of the sequence
M^{\prime}(\gamma)
defined by:

(31)

\widehat{\widehat{\mu}}_{j}=\begin{cases}\mu_{(i,b_{j}+1)}\circ\cdots\circ\mu_{(i,a_{j}-1)}&\text{ if }1<a_{j};\\
\mathrm{id}&\text{ if }a_{j}=1,\end{cases}

and
M^{\prime}(\gamma)=\widehat{\widehat{\mu}}_{\ell}\circ\widehat{\widehat{\mu}}_{\ell-1}\circ\cdots\circ\widehat{\widehat{\mu}}_{1}
.

Proposition 6.25
.

By cancelling repeated mutations and commuting mutations of non-adjacent indices, we have that
M(\gamma)
and
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}
are satisfy

(32)

\overleftarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}}}\circ M(\gamma)(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))=\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})).

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the length of
\underline{w}
and separately handle the two cases of whether the last letter is supported or unsupported in
\gamma
.
When
\underline{w}=(i_{1})
is of length
1
, both sequences are identity, regardless of the choice of
\gamma
. Similarly, when all letters are supported
\gamma=\underline{w}
, then
M(\gamma)=\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}
and
\overleftarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}}}=\overleftarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}
, and both seeds in
32
are equal to
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
.

Now suppose inductively that we have
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{\prime}}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}^{\prime}))=\overleftarrow{\mu_{(\gamma^{\prime})^{+}}}\circ M(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}^{\prime}))
for some
\gamma^{\prime}
a subexpression of a word
\underline{w}^{\prime}
of length
\ell-1
. Let
\gamma=(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma_{\ell})
be one of two subexpressions of
\underline{w}=(\underline{w}^{\prime},i_{\ell})
.
We handle the two cases of
\gamma_{\ell}=i_{\ell}
and
\gamma_{\ell}=-i_{\ell}
separately.

In the first case, when
\gamma_{\ell+1}=i_{\ell}
, we have

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))

\displaystyle=\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle=\overleftarrow{\mu_{(\gamma^{\prime})^{+}}}\circ M(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=\left(\overleftarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}}}\right)\circ\overleftarrow{\mu_{(\gamma^{\prime})^{+}}}\circ M(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=\overleftarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}}}\circ\overleftarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}[\ell]}}\circ M(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=\overleftarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}}}\circ\left(\mu_{(i,b_{\ell}+1)}\circ\dots\circ\mu_{(i,n_{i}-1)}\right)\circ M(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=\overleftarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}}}\circ M(\gamma)(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

where the last line is an application of Lemma
6.24
.

Similarly, when
\gamma_{\ell+1}=-i_{\ell}
we have

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w}))

\displaystyle=\left(\overleftarrow{\mu_{(\gamma^{\prime})^{+}}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{((\gamma^{\prime})^{+},i_{\ell})}}\right)\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{\prime}}}(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=\left(\overleftarrow{\mu_{(\gamma^{\prime})^{+}}}\circ\overrightarrow{\mu_{((\gamma^{\prime})^{+},i_{\ell})}}\right)\circ\left(\overleftarrow{\mu_{(\gamma^{\prime})^{+}}}\circ M(\gamma^{\prime})\right)(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=\overleftarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}}}\circ\left(\mu_{(i,b_{\ell}+1)}\circ\dots\circ\mu_{(i,n_{i}-1)}\right)\circ M(\gamma^{\prime})(\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})),

\displaystyle=\overleftarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}}}\circ M(\gamma).\qed

In
[
1
, §5]
, Ménard’s algorithm is geometrically interpreted as iteratively viewing

\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}^{v}
as a closed subvariety of
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}^{vs_{i}}
for
vs_{i}<v
. The coincidence of the sequence means our procedure gives another geometric interpretation for open Richardson seeds.

Corollary 6.26
.

The seeds for the open Richardson varieties obtained by Ménard’s algorithm
[
30
]
are obtained by iterated pullbacks, quasi-equivalences, and defrostings of
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}}}(\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+}))
under the sequence of rational maps

\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\gamma_{(1)}^{+}}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\gamma_{(2)}^{+}}\dashrightarrow\cdots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}},

followed by the freezing and deletion of the remaining copy of
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}}}(\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+}))
.

Remark 6.27
.

In
[
30
,
1
]
, the sequence
M(\gamma)
of mutations was used in the case that
\gamma
was the left-most subexpression of a reduced word
\gamma^{+}=v\leq w
. The mutation sequence is followed by freezing and deletions of (a mutation of) this copy of
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\gamma^{+}}}(\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+}))
to obtain seeds for the open Richardson variety
\mathring{Z}_{\underline{w}}^{v}
.
In contrast, our seed
\mathbf{s}(\gamma)
for the chart
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
is obtained by the freezing (and isolation via quasi-equivalence) of the vertices in the complement of
\mathbf{s}(\gamma^{+})
.
We expect this to have some interpretation as some form of semi-orthogonal decomposition in the cluster categorification of
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}]
via preprojective algebra modules
[
20
,
27
]
and hope to return to this in future work.

It is unclear to the author whether there is a reasonable categorification of
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}]
such that its maps to
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}]
and from
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}]
can be represented by appropriate functors. The isolated frozens in the seeds for
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma^{+}}
should correspond to indecomposable projectives with no morphisms to the other summands of the cluster tilting object.

Appendix A
Frayed Demazure weaves

In this section we broaden our definition of weaves with frayed strands to define a
frayed weave
, which will have more vertices than was previously allowed. The previous sections did not require the extra vertices we introduce here, but they arise naturally when considering isotopies of fraying vertices. As in previous sections, we initially work in the simply-laced case before showing how to extend to arbitrary finite type by folding arguments.

A.1.
Vertices for frayed weaves

Frayed Demazure weaves will be the analogous combinatorial notion which describe particular local isomorphisms between Bott–Samelson charts. A
frayed Demazure weave
is a planar graph consisting of
\pm I
-coloured edges and vertices of specified valences arising from the Dynkin diagram. The identities corresponding to the vertices in simply-laced type are listed in the following lemma, with each part corresponding to a row in Figure
8
.

Lemma A.1
.

(1)

If
i
and
j
are adjacent, then we have the equalities

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})B_{j}(z_{2})B_{i}(z_{3})

\displaystyle=B_{j}(z_{3})B_{i}(z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2})B_{j}(z_{1}),

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})B_{j}(z_{2})u_{-i}(z_{3})

\displaystyle=u_{-j}(z_{3})B_{i}(z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3})B_{j}(z_{2}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle u_{-i}(z_{1})B_{j}(z_{2})B_{i}(z_{3})

\displaystyle=B_{j}(z_{2}+z_{1}z_{3})B_{i}(z_{3})u_{-j}(z_{1}).

(2)

If
i
and
k
are not adjacent, then we have the equalities

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})B_{k}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=B_{k}(z_{2})B_{i}(z_{1}),

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})u_{-k}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=u_{-k}(z_{2})B_{i}(z_{1}),

\displaystyle u_{-i}(z_{1})B_{k}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=B_{k}(z_{2})u_{-i}(z_{1}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle u_{-i}(z_{1})u_{-k}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=u_{-k}(z_{2})u_{-i}(z_{1}).

(3)

If
i
and
j
are adjacent, then we have the equalities

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})u_{-j}(z_{2})u_{-i}(z_{3})

\displaystyle=u_{-j}(z_{2}z_{3})B_{i}(z_{1}-z_{3})u_{-j}(z_{2}),

\displaystyle u_{-j}(z_{1})B_{i}(z_{2})u_{-j}(z_{3})

\displaystyle=B_{i}\left(z_{2}+\frac{z_{1}}{z_{3}}\right)u_{-j}(z_{3})u_{-i}\left(\frac{z_{1}}{z_{3}}\right),

\displaystyle u_{-i}(z_{1})u_{-j}(z_{2})u_{-i}(z_{3})

\displaystyle=u_{-j}\left(\frac{z_{2}z_{3}}{z_{1}+z_{3}}\right)u_{-i}(z_{1}+z_{3})u_{-j}\left(\frac{z_{1}z_{2}}{z_{1}+z_{3}}\right),\text{ and}

\displaystyle B_{i}(z)

\displaystyle=u_{-\alpha_{i}}(z^{-1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(z)u_{\alpha_{i}}(-z^{-1}),

with the last three only when
z_{3}\neq 0
,
z_{1}+z_{3}\neq 0
, and
z_{1}\neq 0
respectively.

(4)

We have the equalities

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})u_{-i}(z_{2})=B_{i}(z_{1}-z_{2}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle u_{-i}(z_{1})u_{-i}(z_{2})=u_{-i}(z_{1}+z_{2}).

Proof.

These identities can be verified by computations in
SL_{3}
.
∎

Remark A.2
.

This third identity in
3
is the change of Lusztig coordinates for the unipotent cell, and tropicalised in
[
6
, Definition 4.9]
to define the propagation rules for Lusztig cycles.

Figure 8
.
The types of vertices allowed for frayed weaves in simply-laced type, and their effect on the strand labellings. As before,
blue
and
red
strands represent adjacent roots
i
and
j
, and
green
strands to denote a root
k
not adjacent to
i
. The vertices in the first two rows correspond to isomorphisms between charts. Those in the third row correspond to maps which are invertible on an open set given by the non-vanishing of a variable carried by a particular frayed. The fourth row consists trivalent vertices whose maps which reduce the dimension.

Each (generic) horizontal slice of a frayed Demazure weave is a signed word with letters in
\pm I
, and in this way each frayed weave represents a sequence of rational maps between Bott–Samelson charts.
The Demazure product of a signed expression will be the Demazure product of its subsequence of positive entries. In this way, each of the above vertices only (weakly) decreases the Demazure product as move down the weave.

Remark A.3
.

If there are no frayed strands to the right of a fraying vertex, the indeterminacy locus of this rational map is simply the coordinate at that position. However, when there are negative letters to the right, depending on the intermediate word between the two positions, the indeterminacy locus might have other components. See Subsection
B.5
.

Remark A.4
.

The above are the only identities between braid matrices on two roots which give local isomorphisms between Bott–Samelson charts. Indeed, the equality

B_{(-i,-j,i)}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=u_{-i}(z_{1})u_{-j}(z_{2})B_{i}(z_{3})=u_{-j}(z_{1}^{\prime})B_{i}(z_{2}^{\prime})u_{-j}(z_{3}^{\prime})=B_{(-j,i,-j)}(z^{\prime}_{1},z^{\prime}_{2},z^{\prime}_{3})

only holds when
z_{1}=z_{3}^{\prime}=0
and results in
z_{1}^{\prime}=z_{2}
,
z_{2}^{\prime}=z_{3}
.
Similarly, the equality

B_{i}(z_{1})u_{-j}(z_{2})B_{i}(z_{3})=u_{-j}(z_{1}^{\prime})B_{i}(z_{2}^{\prime})u_{-j}(z_{3}^{\prime})

never holds.

Proposition A.5
.

The identities in Lemma
A.1
give rise to Poisson maps between Bott–Samelson charts.

Proof.

First consider the length two relations in part
2
. Let

\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\{i,-i\}\times\{k,-k\}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\gamma}=(\gamma_{2},\gamma_{1}).

In the Bott–Samelson coordinates on
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}
, the maps in from Lemma
A.1

2
are

f:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}},\quad(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto(z_{2},z_{1}).

By the Elek–Lu formula in Lemma
3.8
, since the roots
i
and
k
are nonadjacent, the bracket between the coordinate functions is trivial on both the domain and codomain of this isomorphism, and the map
\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}
is a Poisson map.

Now let
i
and
j
denote adjacent roots in a simply-laced case.
The first two maps
(i,j,i)\to(j,i,j)
and
(i,j,-i)\to(-j,i,j)
in part
1
were previously shown in Propositions
4.6
and
5.2
respectively. The remaining map
(-i,j,i)\to(j,i,-j)
is inverse to the latter map, see Remark
A.6
below, and therefore also Poisson.
The two maps in part
4
are the Poisson map from Lemma
4.10
expressed in their respective charts.

Finally, we handle the new vertices in part
3
.
The last map corresponds to a transition function between adjacent charts on the same Poisson variety and is therefore Poisson.
The remaining vertices can be checked with the formula (
7
) in the same manner as in Propositions
4.6
and
5.2
.
∎

Remark A.6
.

The maps for the vertices
(i,j,i)\to(j,i,j)
,
(i,j,-i)\to(-j,i,j)
, and
(-i,j,i)\to(j,i,-j)
are (Poisson) biregular isomorphisms. The inverse of the first is itself with
i
and
j
swapped, and the latter two are inverse to each other (after swapping
i
and
j
). That is,

\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i)}\cong\mathcal{O}^{(j,i,j)}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{O}^{(-i,j,i)}\cong\mathcal{O}^{(j,i,-j)}.

Similarly, the length two commuting relations are also isomorphisms.
However, the map
\mathcal{O}^{(i,-j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-j,i,-j)}
is only invertible on the open set
z_{3}\neq 0
.

Corollary A.7
.

The Poisson rational (resp. rational) maps
f:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}
in Proposition
A.5
induce Poisson regular (resp. rational) maps

\mathcal{O}^{(\gamma^{\prime},\gamma,\gamma^{\prime\prime})}\to\mathcal{O}^{(\gamma^{\prime},\widetilde{\gamma},\gamma^{\prime\prime})}
, where
\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
are sequences of signed letters.

Proof.

As checked in Lemma
A.1
, the maps
f:\mathcal{O}^{\gamma}\to\mathcal{O}^{\widetilde{\gamma}}
as defined satisfy
\widetilde{m}\circ f=m
. The argument used to prove Proposition
4.9
can be repeated.
∎

A.2.
Non-simply-laced types

As in Section
5.2
, we need vertices with higher valency when the Dynkin diagram is non-simply-laced. As before, we state things for
B_{2}
using the folding from
A_{3}
. The necessary vertices for type
G_{2}
are analogously folded from type
D_{4}
.

Lemma A.8
.

On a rank
2
root subsystem with Cartan submatrix
\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\
-2&2\end{pmatrix}
, when
\underline{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})
, we have the following relations:

\displaystyle B_{(1,2,1,-2)}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=u_{-2}(z_{4})B_{1}(z_{1}-z_{3}z_{4})B_{2}(z_{2}-z_{3}^{2}z_{4})B_{1}(z_{3}),

\displaystyle B_{(2,1,2,-1)}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=u_{-1}(z_{4})B_{2}(z_{1}-2z_{2}z_{4}+z_{3}z_{4}^{2})B_{1}(z_{2}-z_{3}z_{4})B_{2}(z_{3}),

(33)

\displaystyle B_{(1,2,-1,-2)}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=u_{-2}(z_{3}^{2}z_{4})B_{1}(z_{1}-z_{3}z_{4})B_{2}(z_{2}-z_{4})u_{-1}(z_{3}),

(34)

\displaystyle B_{(2,1,-2,-1)}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=u_{-1}(z_{3}z_{4})B_{2}(z_{1}-2z_{2}z_{3}z_{4}+z_{3}z_{4}^{2})B_{1}(z_{2}-z_{4})u_{-2}(z_{3}),

(35)

\displaystyle B_{(1,-2,-1,-2)}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=u_{-2}(\tfrac{z_{2}z_{3}^{2}z_{4}}{z_{2}+z_{4}})B_{1}(\tfrac{z_{1}(z_{2}+z_{4})-z_{3}z_{4}}{z_{2}+z_{4}})u_{-2}(z_{2}+z_{4})u_{-1}(\tfrac{z_{2}z_{3}}{z_{2}+z_{4}}),

(36)

\displaystyle B_{(2,-1,-2,-1)}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=u_{-1}(\tfrac{z_{2}z_{3}z_{4}}{z_{2}+z_{4}})B_{2}(\tfrac{z_{1}(z_{2}+z_{4})^{2}-(2z_{2}+z_{4})z_{3}z_{4}}{(z_{2}+z_{4})^{2}})u_{-1}(z_{2}+z_{3})u_{-2}(\tfrac{z_{2}^{2}z_{3}}{(z_{2}+z_{4})^{2}}),

(37)

\displaystyle B_{(-1,-2,-1,-2)}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=u_{-2}(\tfrac{z_{2}z_{3}^{2}z_{4}}{p_{2}})u_{-1}(\tfrac{p_{2}}{p_{1}})u_{-2}(\tfrac{p_{1}^{2}}{p_{2}})u_{-1}(\tfrac{z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}}{p_{1}}),

(38)

\displaystyle B_{(-2,-1,-2,-1)}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=u_{-1}(\tfrac{z_{2}z_{3}z_{4}}{p_{1}})u_{-2}(\tfrac{p_{1}^{2}}{p_{2}^{*}})u_{-1}(\tfrac{p_{2}^{2}}{p_{1}})u_{-2}(\tfrac{z_{1}z_{2}^{2}z_{3}}{p_{2}^{*}}),

where
p_{1}=z_{1}z_{2}+z_{1}z_{4}+z_{3}z_{4}
,
p_{2}=z_{1}^{2}z_{2}+(z_{1}+z_{3})^{2}z_{4}
, and
p_{2}^{*}=z_{3}z_{4}^{2}+z_{1}(z_{2}+z_{4})^{2}
.
The unfolded weaves of (
33
)–(
36
) are depicted in Figure
9
.

Proof.

These relations can be seen by applying the previous simply-laced identities to an unfolded weave for type
B_{2}
, see Figure
9
. The formulas have been checked by an
SL_{4}
computation in SAGE.
∎

.

\displaystyle B_{(-2,1,2,1)}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle B_{(-1,2,1,2)}(\underline{z})

Example A.10
.

In type
B_{2}
, recall the seed
\mathbf{s}_{0}=\mathbf{s}(1,2,1,2)
which was shown in Example
5.20
. The mutable part of the valued quiver is of mutation type
B_{2}
, and its exchange graph consists of six distinct clusters arranged in a cycle.
We list these in the Table
1
.

\mathbf{s}_{0}

Table 1
.
The six distinct clusters reachable by mutation from
\mathbf{s}_{0}
.

To get the change of coordinates
\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1,2)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(-1,2,1,2)}
, we invert the first coordinate and calculate the change to the rest of the variables. We do this calculation in
\SL_{4}
using the unfolded roots
\widehat{1}=\{1,3\}
and
\widehat{2}=\{2\}
, so the one parameter subgroups are given by

u_{\widehat{1}}(z)=u_{1}(z)u_{3}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}1&z&0&0\\
0&1&0&0\\
0&0&1&z\\
0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad u_{\widehat{2}}(z)=u_{2}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&0\\
0&1&z&0\\
0&0&1&0\\
0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Fraying the first strand and modifying the variables to the right, we calculate that

\displaystyle B_{1}(z_{1})B_{2}(z_{2})B_{1}(z_{3})

\displaystyle B_{2}(z_{4})=u_{-1}(z_{1}^{-1})\alpha_{1}^{\vee}(z_{1})u_{1}(-z_{1}^{-1})B_{2}(z_{2})B_{1}(z_{3})B_{2}(z_{4}),

\displaystyle=u_{-1}(z_{1}^{-1})B_{2}(\tfrac{z_{2}}{z_{1}^{2}})B_{1}(\tfrac{z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}}{z_{1}})B_{2}(z_{1}^{2}z_{4}-2z_{1}z_{3}+z_{2})\alpha_{1}^{\vee}(z_{1})u_{1}(-z_{1}^{-1}).

Observe that the cluster variable
\mu_{2}(z_{2})=z_{1}^{2}z_{4}-2z_{1}z_{3}+z_{2}
appears in the numerator of the fourth coordinate after the change of coordinates
\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1,2)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(-1,2,1,2)}
.
It is thus the second cluster variable under the opening sequence
(1,4,2,3)
.

Also note that the variable
z_{1}^{2}z_{4}+z_{2}^{3}-2z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}
is not in any cluster which is mutation-adjacent to a cluster containing
z_{4}
. However, if we consider the transposition swapping between opening sequences
(1,4,2,3)
and
(4,1,2,3)
we get the two seeds
\mu_{2}(\mathbf{s}_{0})
and
\mu_{1,2,1}(\mathbf{s}_{0})
. These distinct seeds are not related by a one-step mutation.
It might be interesting to compare the cluster exchange graph, its subgraph of weave mutations, and the graph given by transpositions in the opening sequence.

A.3.
Framed flag relations

In
[
6
]
, frayed versions of the identities are constructed to determine how framed labellings propagate in order to construct Lusztig cycles. In this section, we list framed versions of our simply-laced identities.

The following is the version of Lemma
4.4
for frayed strands.

Lemma A.11
.

Suppose we have
g\cdot U\in G/U
a framed flag, and parameters
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
, and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
. If
gu_{-i}(z)\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x)\cdot U=gu_{-i}(z^{\prime})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x^{\prime})\cdot U
, then
z=z^{\prime}
and
x=x^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The element
\bigl(u_{-i}(z)\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x)\bigr)^{-1}u_{-i}(z^{\prime})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x^{\prime})=\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x^{-1})u_{-i}(z^{\prime}-z)\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x^{\prime})
is in the unipotent group
U
if and only if
x^{-1}x^{\prime}=1
and
z^{\prime}-z=0
.
∎

The next statement lists the framed versions of the identities from Lemma
A.1
.

Lemma A.12
.

(1)

When
i
and
j
are adjacent, the following hold:

(a)

Provided that
x_{1}x_{2}=x_{2}^{\prime}x_{3}^{\prime}
and
x_{2}x_{3}=x_{1}^{\prime}x_{2}^{\prime}
, then

B_{i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1})B_{j}(z_{2})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{2})B_{i}(z_{3})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{3})=B_{j}(z_{1}^{\prime})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{1}^{\prime})B_{i}(z_{2}^{\prime})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{2}^{\prime})B_{j}(z_{3}^{\prime})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{3}^{\prime}),

where the
z_{i}^{\prime}
are determined by the
z_{i}
,
x_{i}
, and
x_{i}^{\prime}
.

(b)

Provided that
x_{1}x_{3}=x_{1}^{\prime}x_{2}^{\prime}
and
x_{1}x_{2}=x_{2}^{\prime}x_{3}^{\prime}
, then

B_{i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1})B_{j}(z_{2})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{2})u_{-i}(z_{3})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{3})=u_{-j}(z_{1}^{\prime})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{1}^{\prime})B_{i}(z_{2}^{\prime})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{2}^{\prime})B_{j}(z_{3}^{\prime})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{3}^{\prime}),

where the
z_{i}^{\prime}
are determined by the
z_{i}
,
x_{i}
, and
x_{i}^{\prime}
.

(c)

Provided that
x_{2}x_{3}=x_{1}^{\prime}x_{2}^{\prime}
and
x_{1}x_{2}=x_{1}^{\prime}x_{3}^{\prime}
, then

u_{-i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1})B_{j}(z_{2})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{2})B_{i}(z_{3})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{3})=B_{j}(z_{1}^{\prime})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{1}^{\prime})B_{i}(z_{2})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{2}^{\prime})u_{-j}(z_{3}^{\prime})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{3}^{\prime}).

where the
z_{i}^{\prime}
are determined by the
z_{i}
,
x_{i}
, and
x_{i}^{\prime}
.

(2)

If
i
and
k
are not adjacent, then we have the equalities

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1})B_{k}(z_{2})\alpha_{k}^{\vee}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=B_{k}(z_{2})\alpha_{k}^{\vee}(x_{2})B_{i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1}),

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1})u_{-k}(z_{2})\alpha_{k}^{\vee}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=u_{-k}(z_{2})\alpha_{k}^{\vee}(x_{2})B_{i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1}),

\displaystyle u_{-i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1})B_{k}(z_{2})\alpha_{k}^{\vee}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=B_{k}(z_{2})\alpha_{k}^{\vee}(x_{2})u_{-i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1}),\text{ and }

\displaystyle u_{-i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1})u_{-k}(z_{2})\alpha_{k}^{\vee}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=u_{-k}(z_{2})\alpha_{k}^{\vee}(x_{2})u_{-i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1}).

(3)

When
i
and
j
are adjacent, the following hold:

(a)

Provided that
x_{2}=x_{1}^{\prime}x_{3}^{\prime}
and
x_{1}x_{3}=x_{1}^{\prime}x_{2}^{\prime}
, we have

B_{i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1})u_{-j}(z_{2})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{2})u_{-i}(z_{3})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{3})=u_{-j}(z_{1}^{\prime})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1}^{\prime})B_{i}(z_{2}^{\prime})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{2}^{\prime})u_{-j}(z_{3}^{\prime})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{3}^{\prime}),

where the
z_{i}^{\prime}
are determined by the
z_{i}
,
x_{i}
, and
x_{i}^{\prime}
.

(b)

provided that
x_{1}x_{3}=x_{2}^{\prime}
and
x_{1}x_{2}=x_{1}^{\prime}x_{3}^{\prime}
, we have

u_{-j}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1})B_{i}(z_{2})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{2})u_{-j}(z_{3})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{3})=B_{i}(z_{1}^{\prime})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1}^{\prime})u_{-j}(z_{2}^{\prime})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{2}^{\prime})u_{-i}(z_{3}^{\prime})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{3}^{\prime}),

where the
z_{i}^{\prime}
are determined by the
z_{i}
,
x_{i}
, and
x_{i}^{\prime}
.

(c)

Provided that
x_{2}=x_{1}^{\prime}x_{3}^{\prime}
,
x_{1}x_{3}=x_{2}^{\prime}
, and
z_{1}+z_{3}x_{1}^{-2}x_{2}\neq 0
, we have

u_{-i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1})u_{-j}(z_{2})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{2})u_{-i}(z_{3})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{3})=u_{-j}(z_{1}^{\prime})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1}^{\prime})u_{-i}(z_{2}^{\prime})\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(x_{2})u_{-j}(z_{3}^{\prime})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{3}^{\prime}),

where the
z_{i}^{\prime}
are determined by the
z_{i}
,
x_{i}
, and
x_{i}^{\prime}
.

(d)

We have

B_{i}(z)\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x)=u_{-\alpha_{i}}(z^{-1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(zx)u_{\alpha_{i}}(-z^{-1}x^{-2})
.

(4)

For any root
i
, we have the equalities

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1})u_{-\alpha_{i}}(z_{2})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=B_{i}(z_{1}-x_{1}^{-2}z_{2})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1}x_{2}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle u_{-\alpha_{i}}(z_{1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1})u_{-\alpha_{i}}(z_{2})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=u_{-\alpha_{i}}(z_{1}+x_{1}^{-2}z_{2})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(x_{1}x_{2}).

Proof.

For part
1
,
1a
is from
[
6
, Lemma 3.12]
where we have

z_{1}^{\prime}=z_{3}\frac{x_{1}}{x_{2}},\quad z_{2}^{\prime}=z_{1}z_{3}\frac{x_{1}x_{3}}{x_{2}^{\prime}}-z_{2}\frac{x_{1}^{\prime}}{x_{1}},\quad z_{3}^{\prime}=z_{1}\frac{x_{2}^{\prime}}{x_{1}^{\prime}}.

The remaining two parts are analogously computed. For
1b
, we have

z_{1}^{\prime}=z_{3}\frac{x_{2}}{x_{1}},\quad z_{2}^{\prime}=z_{1}\frac{x_{1}x_{3}}{x_{2}^{\prime}}-z_{2}z_{3}\frac{x_{2}x_{3}}{x_{1}x_{2}^{\prime}},\quad z_{3}^{\prime}=z_{2}\frac{x_{2}}{x_{1}^{\prime}x_{3}^{\prime}},

and for
1c
, we have

z_{1}^{\prime}=z_{2}\frac{x_{1}}{x_{2}}+z_{1}z_{3}\frac{1}{x_{1}},\quad z_{2}^{\prime}=z_{3}\frac{x_{1}x_{3}}{x_{2}^{\prime}},\quad z_{3}^{\prime}=z_{1}\frac{x_{1}x_{1}^{\prime}}{x_{3}x_{3}^{\prime}}.

Simple reflections and one-parameter-subgroups associated to non-adjacent roots commute, so part
2
follows.

For part
3
, subpart
3a
, we have

z_{1}^{\prime}=z_{2}z_{3}\frac{x_{3}x_{3}^{\prime}}{x_{2}^{\prime}},\quad z_{2}^{\prime}=z_{1}x_{1}^{\prime}-z_{3}\frac{x_{2}x_{3}}{x_{1}x_{2}^{\prime}},\quad z_{3}^{\prime}=z_{2}\frac{(x_{1}^{\prime})^{2}}{x_{3}},

for
3b
, we have

z_{1}^{\prime}=z_{2}x_{2}+\frac{z_{1}}{z_{3}}\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{x_{3}^{\prime}},\quad z_{2}^{\prime}=z_{3}\frac{x_{3}^{\prime}}{x_{1}^{2}},\quad z_{3}^{\prime}=\frac{z_{1}}{z_{3}}\frac{x_{1}x_{1}^{\prime}}{x_{3}x_{3}^{\prime}},

and for
3c
, we have

z_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{z_{2}z_{3}x_{1}x_{2}}{z_{1}x_{1}^{2}+z_{3}x_{2}},\quad z_{2}^{\prime}=z_{1}\frac{1}{x_{1}^{\prime}}+z_{3}\frac{x_{3}^{\prime}}{x_{1}^{2}},\quad z_{3}^{\prime}=\frac{z_{1}z_{2}x_{1}^{2}(x_{1}^{\prime})^{2}}{z_{1}x_{1}^{2}x_{3}+z_{3}x_{2}x_{3}}.

The remaining
3
3d
and
4
follow from
SL_{2}
computations.
∎

Example A.13
.

Continuing with Examples
3.12
and
6.15
, we can consider the sequence of rational maps

\mathcal{O}^{(1,2,1,-2)}\dasharrow\mathcal{O}^{(-1,2,1,-2)}\dasharrow\mathcal{O}^{(-1,-2,1,-2)}\dasharrow\mathcal{O}^{(-1,-2,-1,-2)}

\displaystyle\Phi_{(1,2,1,-2)}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})

\displaystyle\mapsto\Phi_{(-1,2,1,-2)}\left(z_{1}^{-1},z_{1}^{-1}z_{2},z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2},\frac{z_{4}}{z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{4})}\right),

\displaystyle\mapsto\Phi_{(-1,-2,1,-2)}\left(z_{1}^{-1},z_{1}z_{2}^{-1},\frac{z_{1}(z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2})}{z_{2}},\frac{z_{4}}{z_{1}(z_{2}-z_{3}z_{4})}\right),

\displaystyle\mapsto\Phi_{(-1,-2,-1,-2)}\left(z_{1}^{-1},z_{1}z_{2}^{-1},\frac{z_{2}}{z_{1}(z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2})},\frac{(z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2})z_{4}}{z_{2}(z_{2}-z_{3}z_{4})}\right).

Note that the presence of the unsupported letter
-2
in the fourth position means that the indeterminacy locus of these maps is larger than the coordinate function at that position.

When using framed labellings, we instead get the sequence

\displaystyle\bigl((z_{1},1),(z_{2},1),(z_{3},1),(z_{4},1)\bigr)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left((\tfrac{1}{z_{1}},z_{1}),(z_{2},1),(\tfrac{z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}}{z_{1}},1),(\tfrac{z_{1}z_{4}}{z_{1}-z_{4}},\tfrac{z_{1}-z_{4}}{z_{1}})\right),

\displaystyle\mapsto\left((\tfrac{1}{z_{1}},z_{1}),(\tfrac{1}{z_{2}},z_{2}),(\tfrac{z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}}{z_{1}},1),(\tfrac{z_{2}z_{4}}{z_{2}-z_{3}z_{4}},\tfrac{z_{2}-z_{3}z_{4}}{z_{2}})\right),

\displaystyle\mapsto\left((\tfrac{1}{z_{1}},z_{1}),(\tfrac{1}{z_{2}},z_{2}),(\tfrac{z_{1}}{z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}},\tfrac{z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}}{z_{1}}),(\tfrac{z_{2}z_{4}}{z_{2}-z_{3}z_{4}},\tfrac{z_{2}-z_{3}z_{4}}{z_{2}})\right).

Note that the pole
z_{1}-z_{4}
from the first map has been cancelled out through the subsequent compositions. Despite it not being a pole of the overall composed map,
A_{1}=z_{1}-z_{4}
is one of our cluster variables. See Example
6.15
.

Appendix B
Frayed weave equivalences and mutations

We have seen that weaves and frayed weaves correspond to sequences of birational maps between Bott–Samelson charts. In
[
6
]
it is shown that two weaves are equivalent if and only if their moduli of flags are the same toric chart in the braid variety. Similarly, two frayed weaves will be equivalent if the sequence of maps has the same indeterminacy loci.

B.1.
Braid move equivalences

We have a similar class of weave equivalences to those in
[
6
]
, but will need more to use on frayed strands.
We declare that any two weaves between signed expressions using invertible braid moves are equivalent. As in
[
6
]
, weaves only using vertices corresponding to local isomorphisms will be equivalent. As in Lemma
5.4
and Figure
5
the two weaves
(i,j,i,-j)\to(i,-i,j,i)\to(i,j,i)
and
(i,j,i,-j)\to(j,i,j,-j)\to(j,i,j)\to(i,j,i)
are equivalent for adjacent roots
i
and
j
. Similarly, for non-adjacent roots
i
and
k
, the weaves
(\pm i,k,-i)\to(\pm i,-i,k)\to(\pm i,k)\to(k,\pm i)
and
(\pm i,k,-i)\to(k,\pm i,-i)\to(k,\pm i)
are equivalent. We can move
k
-coloured strands through
i
-coloured trivalent vertices.

B.2.
Twining equivalences

Any two weaves using only (the two types of) trivalent twining vertices are equivalent. See Figure
10
. The non-equivalence of weave mutation in
[
6
]
arises due to the fraying vertices.

Figure 10
.
Two equivalent frayed weaves from
(1,-1,-1)
to
(1)
.

B.3.
Isotopy of raking rays

In
[
7
, §5]
and
[
6
, §5.1.5.]
, it is checked that the relative heights of trivalent vertices does not affect the labellings on weaves (or the configuration of flags the weave represents). Specifically, the labellings are preserved when hexavalent and trivalent weave vertices are moved by isotopy past a raking ray. The same statement holds for our trivalent, tetravalent and hexavalent vertices involving frayed strands. The proofs for tetravalent and hexavalent vertices are analogous since the corresponding matrix identities come from local isomorphisms.

B.4.
Isotopy of fraying vertices

The equivalences in the previous section do not yet involve the fraying vertices. We now turn our attention to these.
We now look at isotopies which move the fraying vertex past other vertices on the same strand. As with the trivalent vertices,
(i,k)\to(-i,k)\to(k,-i)
and
(i,k)\to(k,i)\to(k,-i)
are equivalent, so we can move
k
-coloured strands through
i
-coloured fraying vertices.

Figure 11
.
Eight equivalences arising from moving a fraying vertex past a tetravalent or hexavalent vertex. For the hexavalent vertices in the bottom row that do not correspond to isomorphisms, note that they shares the same indeterminacy locus as that of the fraying vertex above it.

Proposition B.1
.

For adjacent roots
i,j
, the following pairs of compositions give the same Poisson rational map between Bott–Samelson charts. See Figure
11
.

(1)

\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(j,i,j)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(-j,i,j)}
and
\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-j,i,j)}
;

(2)

\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-j,i,j)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(-j,i,-j)}
and
\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,-i)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(i,-j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-j,i,-j)}
;

(3)

\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(j,i,j)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(j,i,-j)}
and
\mathcal{O}^{(i,j,i)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(-i,j,i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(j,i,-j)}
.

(4)

\mathcal{O}^{(-i,j,i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(j,i,-j)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(j,-i,-j)}
and
\mathcal{O}^{(-i,j,i)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(-i,j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(j,-i,-j)}
,

Furthermore, the
B
-valued modification matrix labelling the rightmost end of the raking ray is equal under both compositions, meaning they can be interchanged within a larger weave without affecting the corresponding moduli of flags.

Proof.

Equalities of the maps can verified by computations in
SL_{3}
with adjacent roots
i=1
and
j=2
. It suffices to check that the composed maps are equal, and that the Borel (or unipotent) matrix corresponding to the raking ray exiting the local pictures are equal. We demonstrate the first calculation, the rest are analogous.

(1)

For the first path, we use

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})

\displaystyle B_{j}(z_{2})B_{i}(z_{3})=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&z_{3}&-1\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&z_{1}&-1\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&z_{3}^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&z_{3}&-1\\
0&0&z_{3}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&z_{1}&-1\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&z_{3}^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}}{z_{3}}&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}z_{3}&0&-1\\
0&1&-\tfrac{z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}}{z_{3}}\\
0&0&z_{3}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&z_{1}&-1\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&z_{3}^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2}}{z_{3}}&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&z_{2}&-1\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}z_{3}&-1&0\\
0&z_{3}^{-1}&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Computing similarly for the second path, we have

\displaystyle B_{i}(z_{1})B_{j}(z_{2})B_{i}(z_{3})

\displaystyle=B_{i}(z_{1})B_{j}(z_{2})u_{-i}(z_{3}^{-1})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(z_{3})u_{i}(-z_{3}^{-1}),

\displaystyle=u_{-j}(z_{3}^{-1})B_{i}(z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3}^{-1})B_{j}(z_{2})\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(z_{3})u_{i}(-z_{3}^{-1}),

and we see that the map between charts and the correction matrix are consistent.

(2)

Computations show that both compositions are equal to the rational map

\Phi_{(i,j,-i)}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto\Phi_{(-j,i,j)}\left(z_{3},z_{1}-z_{2}z_{3},z_{2}^{-1}\right).

The correction matrix on the raking rays are both equal to
\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(z_{2})u_{j}(z_{2}^{-1})
.

(3)

Computations show that both compositions are equal to the rational map

\Phi_{(i,j,i)}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto\Phi_{(j,i,-j)}\left(z_{3},z_{1}z_{3}-z_{2},z_{1}^{-1}\right).

The correction matrix on the raking rays are both equal to
\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(z_{1})u_{j}(z_{1}^{-1})
.

(4)

Computations show that both compositions are equal to the rational map

\Phi_{(-i,j,i)}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto\Phi_{(j,-i,-j)}\left(z_{2}+z_{1}z_{3},z_{3}^{-1},z_{1}z_{3}\right).

The correction matrix on the raking rays are both equal to
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(z_{3})u_{i}(z_{3}^{-1})
.∎

Remark B.2
.

The above means that our maps between Bott–Samelson charts from to the vertices in Figure
8
arise from the
same
rational maps
Z_{(i,k)}\dashrightarrow Z_{(k,i)}
and
Z_{(i,j,i)}\dashrightarrow Z_{(j,i,j)}
restricted to different Bott–Samelson charts.
The vertices in Figure
8
can be thought of as the irreducible ones. Indeed, the restriction of the map to the chart
\mathcal{O}^{(-i,-j,i)}
factors via a fraying
\mathcal{O}^{(-i,-j,i)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(-i,-j,-i)}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{(-j,-i,-j)}
. That is, the identities which failed to hold in Remark
A.4
can hold up to right multiplication by a Borel element which arises from a fraying vertex.

B.5.
Embeddings of moduli of flags for frayed weaves

Consider the two weaves
\mathfrak{w},\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}
corresponding to the compositions
\mathcal{O}^{(i,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(i)}\to\mathcal{O}{(-i)}
and
\mathcal{O}^{(i,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-i,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-i)}
respectively.
A calculation in
SL_{2}
shows that in the Bott–Samelson coordinates, the former map is given by

\Phi_{(i,-i)}(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto\Phi_{(i)}(z_{1}-z_{2})\mapsto\Phi_{(-i)}\left(\tfrac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\right),

while the latter is given by

\Phi_{(i,-i)}(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto\Phi_{(-i,-i)}\left(\tfrac{1}{z_{1}},\tfrac{z_{2}}{z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})}\right)\mapsto\Phi_{(-i)}\left(\tfrac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\right).

The indeterminacy locus of the first path is the vanishing of
z_{1}-z_{2}
, whereas for the second path it is the vanishing of
z_{1}(z_{1}-z_{2})
, which has an extra irreducible component. This gives us an inclusion of
M(\mathfrak{w}^{\prime})\hookrightarrow M(\mathfrak{w})
.
The
B
-valued correction matrix which labels the raking ray is the same in both paths, so there will be no other changes in the indeterminacy locus when we apply this isotopy within a longer signed expression
\gamma
.

Furthermore, we can apply an isotopy to move a fraying vertex past the two remaining types of hexavalent vertices, which results in the fraying vertex moving onto the only remaining solid strand above the hexavalent vertex. These similarly result in embeddings of flag moduli as the indeterminacy locus of the corresponding map gains another irreducible component. These isotopies are shown in Figure
12
.

Proposition B.3
.

When
i,j
are adjacent roots, the following pairs of compositions give the same Poisson rational map between three dimensional Bott–Samelson charts. See Figure
12
.
When the variable at the top of the first strand
z_{1}\neq 0
we have the same statement for the pair,

(1)

\mathcal{O}^{(i,-j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-j,i,-j)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-j,-i,-j)}
and
\mathcal{O}^{(i,-j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-i,-j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-j,-i,-j)}
;

and similarly when
z_{2},z_{3}\neq 0
, we have the same for the pair

(2)

\mathcal{O}^{(-i,j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(j,-i,-j)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-j,-i,-j)}
and
\mathcal{O}^{(-i,j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-i,-j,-i)}\to\mathcal{O}^{(-j,-i,-j)}
.

Proof.

Equalities of the maps can verified by computations in
SL_{3}
with
i=1
and
j=2
being the two adjacent roots. It suffices to check that the composed maps are equal, and that the Borel element labelling the raking ray exiting the local pictures are equal.

(1)

Computations show that both compositions are equal to the rational map

\Phi_{(i,-j,-i)}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto\Phi_{(-j,-i,-j)}\left(z_{2}z_{3},(z_{1}-z_{3})^{-1},(z_{1}-z_{3})z_{2}\right),

and both correction matrices on the raking rays equal to
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}(z_{1}-z_{3})u_{i}((z_{1}-z_{3})^{-1})
.

(2)

Calculations show that the two maps are equal to the rational map

\Phi_{(-i,j,-i)}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto\Phi_{(-j,-i,-j)}\left(\tfrac{z_{3}}{z_{1}+z_{2}z_{3}},z_{1}+z_{2}z_{3},\tfrac{z_{1}}{z_{2}(z_{1}+z_{2}z_{3})}\right).

In both paths, the correction matrix is given by
U=\alpha_{j}^{\vee}(z_{2})u_{j}(z_{2}^{-1})
.∎

Figure 12
.
Embeddings of weave moduli for other isotopies of fraying vertices.

B.6.
Mutations of frayed weaves

In
[
6
]
, weave mutation is a move between the two possible trees from
(i,i,i)
to
(i)
. From the relation of a weave and its opening sequence constructed in the proof of
[
7
, Theorem 5.17]
, we can also think of this as changing the order of the opening sequence of a weave.

Lemma B.4
.

Any weave mutation can be realised as swapping the order of two consecutive frayings in its opening sequence.

Remark B.5
.

The converse is not true. See Example
A.10
where applying a transposition to an opening sequence yields a non-adjacent seed.

Figure 13
.
Mutation of Demazure weaves from
[
6
]
compared against the frayed weaves arising from commuting the order of fraying vertices.

Note however, that our embeddings of weave moduli arising from isotopy of frayed vertices gives us an interpretation of this swapped ordering of fraying sequences. Namely, the frayed weaves
\mathfrak{w}:(i,i)\to(-i,i)\to(-i,-i)
and
\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}:(i,i)\to(i,-i)\to(-i,-i)
are closely related to mutation, see Figure
13
. Notably, the moduli of flags
M(\mathfrak{w}^{\prime})
is the intersection of the cluster torus
M(\mathfrak{w})
, and its mutation adjacent torus.

Conjecture B.6
.

For any word
\underline{w}
, the frayed weave consisting only of fraying vertices

\displaystyle\mathfrak{w}_{\mathrm{rev}}:(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell-1},i_{\ell})

\displaystyle\to(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\ell-1},-i_{\ell})\to(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,-i_{\ell-1},-i_{\ell}),

\displaystyle\to\cdots\to(i_{1},-i_{2},\dots,-i_{\ell-1},-i_{\ell})\to(i_{1},-i_{2},\dots,-i_{\ell-1},-i_{\ell}),

corresponding to the reversed opening sequence
(\ell,\ell-1,\dots,2,1)
has its moduli of flags
M(\mathfrak{w}_{\mathrm{rev}})
equal to the the intersection of all cluster tori in
\mathcal{O}^{\underline{w}}
reached along the maximal green sequence
\overrightarrow{\mu_{\underline{w}}}
applied to
\mathbf{s}(\underline{w})
.
